* **CAF:** Development Bank of Latin America is a multilateral development bank that promotes a sustainable development model through credit operations, non-concessional loans, and technical assistance. It finances projects in the public and private sectors of Latin America. CAF is a Regional Direct Access (RDA) entity to the GCF. It will hold the role of Accredited Entity and Executing Entity.

* **Corporación Financiera Nacional B.P. - CFN (National Financial Corporation):** The CFN is an autonomous public institution that finances projects in the productive and strategic sectors of Ecuador, through multiple financial and non-financial mechanisms. It works within the framework of the National Development Plan and the guidelines of the National Government's programs aimed at stabilizing and developing the economy, becoming a decisive agent for achieving the results undertaken.

* **Corporación Nacional de Finanzas Populares y Solidarias (CNF):** The CNF is an autonomous public institution created by Law No. 1096 of the Organic Law of the Popular and Solidary Economy. The Corporation will have the function to promote, coordinate, and supervise the development of mechanisms by the Inter-Institutional Committee to the organizations covered by the Organic Law of the Popular and Solidary Economy. It will also develop complementary mechanisms for which purpose it will exercise the functions that will be set forth in the corresponding regulations. The CNF is a public financial institution and second-tier development financial institution in the provision of products, financial services, and other complementary services to organizations covered by the Popular and Solidary Economy Act, contributing to their inclusion and consolidation in the productive and solidary economy.

* **FAO Ecuador:** The Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) is a specialized agency of the United Nations that leads international efforts to defeat hunger. Its goal is to eliminate poverty and make sure everyone has enough food to lead active lives with over 194 member states, including Ecuador.

* **WWF Ecuador:** World Wildlife Fund (WWF) is one of the largest international nature conservation organizations, working in nearly 100 countries. In Ecuador, WWF works through a network of partners and local communities to protect wildlife and their habitats, promote sustainable development, and mobilize society for sustainability. WWF Ecuador has 7 offices across the country.

* **Concordia SPV:** Will be established for the purposes of implementing the Concordia Project sponsored by Gran Solar and TotalEren, the concessionaire of the solar power plant in Guayas province, Ecuador. GranSolar is a leading Ecuadorian company founded in 2012 by a visionary group of entrepreneurs with the aim to promote renewable energy projects in the country. Since 2014 the company owns the largest solar plant in Ecuador. Total Eren was created in 2015 as a joint venture between Total and EREN Group. It finances, builds and operates over the long-term renewable energy power plants under concession agreements with the Government of Ecuador. The Concordia SPV is in operation or under construction and has over 4,300 MW of projects under development. The company is part of the French multinational TotalEren and its subsidiary Nexo Energía Holding Company ("NHC" or "NonEnergía"), thereby diversifying its project portfolio and strengthening its presence in southern Europe in particular.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL 3.0 | PAGE OF 152

The Galápagos Islands are part of the diversity of Ecuador. They lie about 1,000 kilometers from the Ecuadorian coast in the Pacific Ocean. The diversity and uniqueness of its species, its volcanic origin, and its geological dynamics make these islands be considered a living laboratory of evolutionary processes, added to the fact that it evolved conditions for the development of unique ecosystems.

This makes Galápagos a unique and globally important site for the common heritage of humanity (declared a Natural Heritage Site by UNESCO in 1978) and a key area for climate change adaptation. The islands are home to about 33,000 people and receive about 106,987 tourists annually from national Ecuadorian territory.

The Galápagos Islands have been declared a World Biosphere Reserve by UNESCO in recognition of their unique natural heritage and nature-based tourism, which are highly vulnerable to global environmental changes and have limited capacity to adapt. The tourism sector is the main economic activity on the islands and its development is based on the conservation of natural resources.

The islands are home to about 33,000 people and receive about 106,987 tourists annually from national Ecuadorian territory. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5 OF 152

## Sustainability mechanisms for climate resilience and low emissions livelihoods: this component will strengthen the response of local livelihoods and population through educational and communicational programs, and increasing capacity to access financing for investments in energy efficiency technologies and adaptation measures.

Planning instruments. It will support the development of sustainability mechanisms such as an ecotourism certification scheme (GCF project), a marine protected area management plan (MPA) and a tourism sector strategy, which will guarantee the sustainability of the programme actions in the long term.

Activities: The activities will be implemented on three islands: Santa Cruz, San Cristóbal, Isabela (Floreana), in High Ecological Value Areas (HEVAS), including the Galápagos Marine Reserve (GMR).

The Programme complements government efforts led by the National Government and by the Governing Council of the Galápagos Islands (GCIG) and the Galápagos National Park Directorate (GNPD), the tourism sector (hotels, restaurants, operators), the local communities and civil society. The programme will be implemented in three islands: Santa Cruz, San Cristóbal and Isabela (Floreana). 470 beneficiaries from the tourism sector will have access to finance for investing in energy-efficient technologies and direct emission reduction measures. 150 households will benefit from adaptation measures implementation. 2,300 people will be trained on climate change awareness and mitigation activities. 600 students of local schools, 400 teachers, 70 institutions, technology providers, specialists from key local agencies, ship owners, port operators, local people will be trained to implement the adaptation measures in their communities. The Programme will also provide technical assistance for developing activities for climate action. The Programme will enhance climate resilience in 19,000 hectares of agricultural areas, and 25,000 ha of natural ecosystems. It will reduce CO2 emissions by 348,760 tons per year (considered as resource use avoided), which is equivalent to the annual emissions from 75,750 cars or the electricity consumption of a city with a population of the islands (33,000 people) and the more than 106,997 annual tourist visits per year, considered as resource use avoided. The Programme will also reduce CO2 emissions by 48 tons per year from the energy sector, which is equivalent to planting 5,280 trees or using a public transport system for 3 years. It will also reduce CO2 emissions by 176 tons per year (considered as resource use avoided) from the waste sector, which is equivalent to cleaning and incinerating 49 tons of garbage per day in the mainland, and enjoy better air quality by reducing the generation of electricity in thermoelectric plants.

The Programme will have a total volume of USD 117.5 million. This includes USD 30.54 million from GCF reimbursable funding, USD 30,230 million in euros from CAF, USD 18.90 million by equity from the Conodorus PV plant tied to the project, USD 6.17 million from the Galápagos Islands Government (GIG), USD 5.4 million from the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and USD 33.07 million in grants from GCF. The USD 1.66 million in grants for Programme Management and a USD 2,890,000 grant for the Project Implementation Unit.

A portion of the reimbursable funding from the GCF and CAF will fund the centralised energy project through a Loan Agreement with the Galápagos Islands Government (GIG) and the Conodorus PV plant tied to the project. The Central Development Bank Corporation Financiera Nacional (CDBF), Corporación Nacional de Finanzas Populares y Gestoras (CNFPyG) and the GCF will provide USD 30,230 million in euros from CAF, USD 6.17 million by equity from the Conodorus PV plant tied to the project, USD 5.4 million from the GIG, USD 33.07 million in grants from GCF and USD 1.66 million in grants for Programme Management and a USD 2,890,000 grant for the Project Implementation Unit.

The Programme will be Implemented by CAF as the Accredited Entity, who will also hold the role of Executive Entity in particular activities, and will co-work with the Executing Entities WFP, FAO, CONAPIS y CNF. The Government of Ecuador (GIE) will provide USD 30.54 million from GCF reimbursable funding, USD 18.90 million by equity from the Conodorus PV plant tied to the project, USD 6.17 million from the GIG and USD 5.4 million from the GCF. B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

# B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## 1. The climate context of the Galápagos Islands is shaped by global trends and future scenarios, impacts on marine and terrestrial resources and on local livelihoods and mitigation opportunities. Please refer to Annex 2 for comprehensive information.

### 2. The Galápagos climate is a product of the interaction of oceanic currents surrounding the islands and the winds from the southeast. The influence of currents and winds is governed by interactions of the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) and the Walker Circulation.

## 3. The unique marine and terrestrial ecosystems of the Galápagos Islands are highly vulnerable to climate change because of climate-induced increased temperatures, increased rainfall seasonality, extreme weather events, sea level rise, and changes in oceanographic conditions such as warming surface temperature and sea level rise. Consequently, inhabitants and their livelihoods such as agriculture, fisheries, tourism, and water supply will be affected by these changes.

## 4. Marine-climate drivers of environmental change related to tourism are all impacts inside the marine space: water contamination and wastewater mismanagement; tourist sites and trails overuse and plant and animal disturbance; and improper waste disposal. These activities are influenced by local regulations and management practices, and illegal, unreported, and unregulated fishing. In all sectors, the introduction of invasive species is of great severity.

## 5. Temperature: Islands have warmed by about 0.6°C since the early 1900s (mean land surface temperature).

### 6. Precipitation: In contrast to this increasing trend precipitation records from 1881 to 2017 suggest a decreasing trend across the archipelago, particularly in arid coastal areas. Clearly, the first two decades of the twentieth century were wetter than any other period since then. The most recent decade shows an increasing trend in precipitation; the records from 2002 to 2017 suggest the precipitation pattern has changed and that the Galápagos Islands are now experiencing a more humid climate. However, many of the changes from 1982 to 1983 and 1997 to 1998, have influenced the time series and prevented a clear interpretation of collective trends.

### 7. Sea Surface Temperature (SST): Has increased at a rate of 0.06°C per year over recent decades. Regional SST has increased by about 0.5°C since the early 1900s, with some areas showing increases as high as 2°C. This suggests that the equatorial Pacific has warmed 0.6–0.8°F over the last 40 years and that greater increases in SST are expected for the future.

### 8. El Niño and La Niña: ENSO is the dominant mode of global climate variability on an inter-annual time scale that strongly impacts the Galápagos. Evidence suggests that ENSO events have increased in intensity and frequency over recent decades, particularly since the mid-1970s.

### Climate change projections (methodology and results in Annex 2 Section A and Appendix 5.1)

#### 9. Global circulation models (GCMs) generally project warmer and wetter future conditions. Temperature is expected to increase by about 3°C over land areas of the Galápagos Islands for the end-of-century time horizon. This increase will be heterogeneous across the islands, with humid zones in the western islands showing greater increases than arid coastal areas.

#### 10. Precipitation trends are more difficult to project because they depend on a number of factors such as warming trends compared to the eastern Galápagos region. Precipitation projections also suggest a relative increase in precipitation for the wet season and a decrease during the dry season, although there is some seasonal variation.

#### 11. For the decades between 2040 and 2060,

These increases in temperature and precipitation will be accompanied by augmented dry and wet extremes, which would ultimately translate into more severe heatwaves and floods in the region.**

10. The Eastern Tropical Pacific (ETP) is expected to have increased SSTs due to global warming. This increase in SSTs has been linked with changes in the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) cycle and associated climate change on the ocean circulation dynamics in the Galápagos Islands projects an increase of a near 2°C rising trend in ETP SST over the next century, which is expected to be accompanied by more frequent and intense El Niño events throughout the twenty-first century. Continued increases in sea surface and air temperature coupled with more intense rainfall will likely transform the current climate system in the Galápagos Islands with increased seasonality and stronger spatial heterogeneity.

11. The studies emphasize the need to incorporate uncertainty and robust/negotiated approaches as the basis for management of marine resources, especially in light of the projected changes in oceanographic conditions. These strategies must be both robust in the face of a wide range of potentially uncertain climate conditions.


## Modelled impacts on marine ecosystems (details in Annex 2. Section 7.1 and 3)

### Impacts on marine resources (details in Annex 2. Section 7.1 and 3)

Upwelling areas could be reduced because of climate induced ocean warming. Past reductions in the upwelling area have been linked with declines in fish biomass, which has had a negative impact on the food chain through fish populations and up to marine birds, mammals and reptiles. The warmer water is less nutrient rich than cooler waters and thus reduces primary productivity in the Galápagos and the marine ecosystem consequently becomes diminished (fish abundance and distribution are reduced). This will have an effect on the archipelago, leading to community-level reductions in biomass. A critical and long-term impact of climate variability and future change will be the loss of biodiversity through species extinction and invasion.

Invasive species: Non-indigenous species (NIS) can provide reduction of the functional diversity of the resident species assemblies by removing key organisms, which may have overall implications for ecosystem functioning. The introduction of NIS to the Galápagos Islands has already had a high NIS impact biodiversity and society, including ecosystem services such as fisheries and nature-based tourism. For example, in 1982, the brown algae (Sargassum muticum) was introduced into the Galápagos Islands via the ship Santa Cruz. This species of green algae competes with native species for space and forms extensive mats changing the structure and function of intertidal communities. The introduction of NIS to the Galápagos Islands will also have variable effects on fish species; for example, positive changes in temperature are expected to increase the growth rate of some species such as the yellowhead jawfish (Opistognathus aurifrons) that has a relatively short life expectancy and is known to be vulnerable to disease. However, other species may be negatively affected by climate change, including the Galápagos puffer fish (Arothron hispidus) which are expected to have reduced growth rates due to increased water temperatures. The introduction of NIS to the Galápagos Islands has already had a high NIS impact on biodiversity and society, including ecosystem services such as fisheries and nature-based tourism.

Ecosystem responses would be very different if there was a change in the baseline climatic conditions or to the frequency or intensity of very wet or very dry years, all of which are possible under global climate change. Anomalies in rainfall and temperature have resulted in substantial impacts in the terrestrial ecosystems. Increased rainfall and productivity can create optimal conditions for invasive species such as the yellowhead jawfish (Opistognathus aurifrons) to dominate community structure of mid and ecosystems, making them more susceptible to colonization by invasive species. This will have a negative impact on the Galápagos Islands' biodiversity and consequently the decline of tropical island birds. One highland species that may be particularly vulnerable is the Galápagos penguin (Spheniscus mendiculus) which has a short life expectancy, suggesting that the short life expectancy of this and other Spheniscus species makes them vulnerable to persistent, long-term disturbances. This increase in the prevalence of pathogens and parasites during rainy conditions can

# Impacts on Livelihoods: Agriculture, Fishing, and Tourism

## Impacts on Livelihoods (details in Annex Section B.2)

### 14. The agricultural area in Galápagos extends over 2.4% of the island's territory (19,010 ha), distributed in 755 farming units, of which 83% are family farms (less than 5 ha.), and 30% are managed by women. All four inhabited islands have agricultural land, but only Santa Cruz has a significant amount of arable land. The majority of the agricultural area is designated for agricultural use. Inhabitants of the highlands of the Galápagos use their land for three general activities: agriculture (65%), livestock farming (20%), and other uses such as tourism (15%). Agricultural productivity in the Galápagos Islands is influenced by several factors, including temperature and precipitation.

### Impacts on Fishing: Small-Scale Fisheries (SSF) (details in Annex Section B.4)

#### 15. SSF is a strategic sector for the economy, food security, and sustainable development of the Galápagos human population. The total catch from SSF in 2018 was approximately 376 tons, with an average price per kilogram of $9.00. Approximately 40% of this catch is consumed locally on the islands.

### Impacts on Tourism: Nature-based tourism (details in Annex Section B)

#### 16. Galápagos plays a key role in Ecuador's tourism industry as it is the main tourist destination in Ecuador and the most visited destination by foreign tourists, accounting for approximately 40% of all visitors to the country. The Gross Domestic Product of Galápagos was US$256.1 million in 2019, where tourism contributed 65.5% of this figure in the archipelago. Therefore, the tourism sector is a major economic driver for the islands and their inhabitants.

#### 7. Impacts on Tourism: Nature-based tourism (details in Annex Section B)

# Key climate change and adaptation measures

## Main impacts

### Nature-based solutions

- **Increase in Sea Surface Temperature (SST):**

## B

### Mitigation opportunities.

**17. The major opportunities to reduce GHG emissions in Galapagos are related to the adoption of renewable energy generation and energy efficiency technologies.**

Electricity (details in Annex 2: Section 9.3):

* **Electrical power generation:** Electricity is highly dependent on imported fossil fuels; grids are inefficient, distributed renewable energy generation is not the mainstream, and there is important room to promote the adoption of energy efficient appliances.
* **Electricity generation:** Electricity is generally highly dependent on imported fossil fuels.

The electric power generation system has four isolated grids: thermoelectric plants are the main sources of power generation. The largest plant in Galapagos is the Guaytamba Power Plant, which uses diesel (ELECAGALPACOS), and in addition to consumption associated with ship transport to islands, this represented emissions worth 35,415 tons CO2e/year. In 2007, the first wind power plant was installed on Santa Cruz Island, generating electricity that constitutes additional GHG emissions associated with transport and adds to the environmental and social risks associated with fossil fuel consumption.

In 2007 under "the Zero Fossil Fuels in Galapagos Islands," it introduced the first renewable energy power plants. As of 2016, there were 35 MW installed capacity for electricity generation from renewable sources (including consumption by 1 million gallons in 2016). Since 2007, solar and wind projects have been developed proving their effectiveness in Galapagos Islands. Other renewable generation technologies have been analyzed, including their development in the short and medium term, such as the case of geothermal energy available only where there is

# The problem

24. Today, Galápagos main livelihoods (agriculture, small scale fishing, and tourism), are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. These sectors are also the most important for providing food security, employment opportunities, and environmental services; they need to increase their resilience to climate change; and at the same time, they are the main sectors through which Galápagos contributes to global GHG emissions.

25. The context of these three main livelihoods is complex, given their dependence on:

- The marine and terrestrial ecosystems services, in terms of food, water, and natural-based tourism attractions.
- Both agriculture and fishing depend heavily on fossil fuels (e.g., diesel for tractors, boats, generators, refrigeration). Nevertheless, agriculture and tourism can significantly contribute to emissions reductions. Agriculture can have a significant impact through the use of renewable energy sources such as solar power or wind power instead of fossil fuel-based energy (machinery, refrigeration). Also, the tourism sector (hotels, restaurants, other tourism operations) can reduce its GHG emissions by using more efficient energy and by reducing imported food consumption; not only through more efficient use of energy and the deployment of renewable energy but also because less food will be imported. The food that is locally produced or imported will no longer have to be transported from the mainland, with its consequent associated emissions.

26. As described in Section B.1, climate risks severely exacerbate the threats posed by the divers of environmental change and human activities on the Galápagos ecosystems. Climate change impacts are expected to affect all three livelihoods: agriculture production, water deficit, pests, loss of environmental services of native ecosystems (e.g., pollination, seed dispersal), increased vulnerability, and general impact on the ecosystems that are the main attraction for the nature-based tourism sector.

27. Galápagos' livelihoods also contribute to global GHG emissions. The small scale fisheries sector does not represent a large share of total GHG emissions in the Galápagos Islands (around 1% of total emissions). However, fishing is an important source of food security and employment for many local communities.

28\. The barriers that are common to the whole Programme are related to access to accessible finance, to the low capacity of the Galapagos institutions to drive transformational change, insufficient technology knowledge for climate adaptation and mitigation measures, lack of technical assistance for project preparation, implementation, and monitoring, and lack of public awareness and engagement.

29\. The barrier that is common to all sectors in Galapagos is related to access to accessible finance, as discussed in the financial economic context and analysis (please refer to Section D.6 of this FP, Annex 3, and appendix 4.1 of Annex Z).

For instance, the centralized PV generation project is feasible only with the concessionary of the GCF, since under conventional financing conditions, it would be unviable due to high costs and low revenues.

A project is not funded. Economic crisis due to COVID-19, or state investments in energy, low foreign investment, lack of public awareness and engagement, and lack of technical assistance for project preparation, implementation, and monitoring are the main barriers related to this investment such as the Conceptual project.

31\. In all sectors, there is a lack of accessible finance for investing in distributed renewable energy, energy efficiency, climate resilient and low-carbon development projects. The financial schemes that exist do not cover the needs of the different sectors or the specific schemes. Also, the local authorities in charge of the management of ecosystems, do not count with funding to invest in these areas.

32\. Barriers related to knowledge and technical capacity are present in all sectors as well: banks, private investors, the tourism, agriculture and SFs sectors do not know how to design and implement the climate investments necessary for their development. The financial institutions lack the technical capacity to comply with the technical requirements of the ecocertification scheme; farmers face knowledge barriers related to invasive species management in their crops and livestock production systems, as well as the protection of ecosystems; they all face barriers related to the lack of information on climate change impacts on their activities.

33\. Local communities are not aware of the need for adaptation and mitigation measures. They do not have innovative approaches to control and eradicate invasive species, active conservation approaches and best practices in the management of natural resources. The local communities also lack knowledge about how to mainstream climate change in their planning, and to design financial sustainability strategies and a locally managed ecosystem approach.

34\. To reach resilient terrestrial and marine ecosystems, other barriers related to low-quality information and lack of monitoring systems to assess impacts of adaptation actions are highly relevant.

35\. The tourism sector is also affected by the loss of biodiversity, lack of connection of the local production to the tourism value chain, among others, which leads them to farm lands abandonment.

Finally, the local ecological community faces barriers related to the lack of articulation to incorporate the climate change adaptation and mitigation measures in their livelihoods with technologies and practices that increase terrestrial resilience. The main barrier is the lack of knowledge about how to mainstream climate change in the context of different sectors, and Section 11 for further detail on the barriers identified and how they will be addressed.

The goal

37\. The Programme has the goal of promoting a transformational change towards a self-sufficient island system in which livelihoods are developed under a low-carbon model and greater capacity to adapt to climate changes.

Programme goal statement: "If the financial, technical and institutional barriers to the dislocation of low carbon energy systems and technologies are removed, and if the necessary knowledge is available for adaptation and mitigation measures in their livelihoods with technologies and practices that increase terrestrial resilience, then it would be possible to effect a transformational change towards self-sufficiency and low-carbon resilient development pathways. The main objective of this programme is to provide financial and non-financial resources to help reduce barriers to investment in mitigation and adaptation projects in the Galapagos Islands.

According to the logic of the proposed intervention, changes in the energy system would be achieved through the promotion of a transformational change towards self-sufficiency and low-carbon resilient development pathways. This would be done by providing financial and non-financial resources for the development of renewable energy systems and energy efficiency technologies, as well as technical assistance for the preparation of energy projects, project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, and capacity building in the Galapagos Islands. The monitoring of such projects by local banks. In this way, these interventions would help reduce the vulnerable livelihoods dependence and that of the entire Galapagos system on external sources and reduce greenhouse gas

## B.3. Project/programme description (max 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

- The Project has as its goal to achieve a transformational change towards a self-sustaining island system in which livelihoods are developed under a low carbon and climate-resilient model. The main livelihoods of the Galápagos Islands are tourism and fishing. This project will provide a framework for sustainable development through which the Galápagos Islands can find a way to significantly reduce their GHG emissions.

**Page 23**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 109 OF 152

## Territorial and programmatic approach of a cross-cutting proposal

The Programme has the objective of providing financial and non-financial resources to help reduce barriers to emission mitigation and adaptation actions, and to foster behavioral change by the agriculture, tourism and transport sectors that currently have limited access to finance and a significant lack of knowledge on how to make climate change impact assessments possible. The Programme seeks to transform the Galápagos community towards a low-carbon economy through the implementation of projects and activities that will reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and to establish mechanisms such as certification schemes, carbon markets, etc., for the sustainability of the Programme actions in the long-term.

## Activities and components

In implementing these objectives, the Programme will use a combination of funding sources, including loans, grants, and equity, through three mutually reinforcing and interlinked components (1) Energy matrix change in the Galápagos islands; (2) Climate-smart agriculture and tourism in the Galápagos Islands; and (3) Sustainable mechanisms for climate resilience and low emissions livelihoods.

### Territorial and programmatic approach of a cross-cutting proposal

The Programme has been designed based on a market study. Please refer to Annex 2 Appendix 4.1 for details about the methodology used in the analysis of the Galápagos Islands. Also, Annex 2 section 13 includes the market studies along with the description of the activities.

### Programmatic approach

The Programme has been prioritized through a multi-criteria analysis methodology that considers potential impact, feasibility level, and potential paradigm shift. Please refer to Annex 2 Section 10 "Analysis of alternatives".

## The Programme resources

### Direct beneficiaries of the Programme are:

- The 21,525 inhabitants of the islands who will become more resilient
- And will be part of a low-carbon development, based on the actions of the Programme, and the more than 106,987 tourists that visit the Galápagos Islands each year.
- More than 470 beneficiaries from the tourism sector will have access to finance for investing in energy-efficient technologies and equipment. This includes hotels, restaurants, tour operators, and other businesses.
- More than 1,000 fishing households (3,000 persons) will benefit from the adaptation measures implementation. Technical assistance will be provided by local government agencies, non-governmental organizations, research institutions, technology providers, specialists from key local agencies, ship owners, students; local people will be trained in climate change awareness and adaptation activities.
- The Programme will also provide training to 100 teachers and 250 students on climate change awareness-raising activities for climate action. The Programme will enhance climate resilience in 19,000 hectares of agricultural land.

### Types of direct beneficiaries

The types of direct beneficiaries of the Programme resources are summarized in the table below. Please refer to Annex 2 Section 13 "Market studies" and Annex 2 Section 14 for a detailed description of the climate impacts they face. Their eligibility is defined in the description of each activity (Annexes section 12).

### Programme Financial structure
The financial structure has been designed as an implementation mechanism which will benefit directly to the largest number of people in Galápagos, different livelihoods: farmers, fishermen, and the tourist operators as well as the financial institutions.

# Programme Grant

The grant will support capacity building, technical assistance, education, awareness-raising activities, as well as direct financial support to projects in key eco-tourism and project sectors (e.g., agriculture, aquaculture, co-management of marine resources, renewable energy, and tourism sectors). The grant will support projects targeting the people of the Galápagos Islands. It will contribute to the achievement of the Fund’s goals by increasing the understanding of how the Programme’s holistic approach can drive the circular economy and last-mile delivery.

## One of the main objectives of the grant is to accompany the implementation of bankable projects so that the Galápagos PV Project (Conopolius) can be implemented in a timely manner. The collaboration with financial institutions in the Galápagos, such as the CFN, CONAFIPS and local banks, to ensure the monitoring and sustainability of the project.

### Conopolius Centralized Power Generation Project

Through the Ministerial Agreement of June 16, 2020, the start of the Public Selection Process was authorized, to execute the concession contract for the construction and operation of the Conopolius Project, for a period of twenty-five (25) years from the signing of the concession contract.

The Agency for the Regulation and Control of Energy and Non-Renewable Natural Resources reserved a reserve of 10% of the total amount of the tender. The group that won the tender was Granolar+Total Eren, which is composed by five companies authorized in August 2020; only the group submitted a technical offer, so, in April, the Technical Commission recommended the awarding of the concession contract to this group. This recommendation enabled the association Granolar+Total Eren for the next phase of the PVS corresponding to the opening of the economic tender.

The 25th of August 2021 the Ministry of Energy and Non-Renewable Natural Resources declared the tender winner of the Conopolius Project. The 9th of September the Ministry notified Granolar+Total Eren the organization was awarded of the tender.

* A Trust Agreement will be signed between the Government of Ecuador and the association Granolar+Total Eren, for the implementation of the Conopolius PV Project. This agreement will establish the framework for project management.
* In the technical aspect, it will increase the coverage, quality, and sustainability of energy generation in Galápagos Islands.

The Trust aims to improve aid efficiency by consolidating under a single agreement using a standardized set of financial terms and conditions. The Trust will be an independent legal entity that will manage the project with its own financing by financial and technical partners of the project defined within the context of the Conopolius project.
* The parties involved in the trust are as follows:
    * Project company: Granolar+Total Eren, which is the tender winner. It transfers the ownership of assets (solar panels, Eolic engines and towers, etc.) to the trust fund.
    * Tender the tender Granolar+Total Eren
* Public Sector: By law, this party cannot transfer ownership of its assets. However, it can provide the Trust with the project site, technical support, and other services. In addition, this party would be the Trust customer (this aspect will be explained in detail in subsequent sections).
* RE plants: Energy producers owned by the Trust.
* The Trust will solely use the loan-processes considered for the Conopolius PV Project. It is an independent legal entity based on Ecuadorian law and subject to a NOC from SPV and SPV’s own collection/payment orders as required by the project and as permitted under the trust's operating rules.

## Financing the Conopolius Project

* CAF will be the financier.
* CAF will sign a Loan Agreement with the Conopolius PV Project tender winner company (the "Conopolius SPV") to finance the construction of the solar power energy plant. Please refer to Annex 2 section 3.0 for further details on the bidding process.

### Galapagos' Climate Credit Line (GCCL)

Several of the Programme's investments will be implemented through a credit line channelled through CFN and/or GCF. The Galapagos Islands are served by two banks: Banco de Galápagos and Banco del Pacífico, because they are the only two banks present in the islands. Please refer to Annex 2 section 13 for details. The activities that include access to credit will be implemented through the Galapagos Climate Credit Line (GCCL). Details of these activities are described in Annex 2 Section 12. Access to these credits will be enhanced by ensuring that the GCF concessional funding is used as a co-financing mechanism, and by providing technical assistance through the Technical Assistance activity. Access to the credit line will be assessed based on the proposed projects' technical quality, with the aim of reaching the target percentages of women accessing credit (see section 8.4). This will be done in conjunction with the Gender Action Plan (GAP – Annex B) to reach the target percentages of women accessing credit (see system 2.1.3.2). The process for assessing projects and providing technical assistance is described below. This process will be led by the Programme's safeguarders and gender specialist (see section 8.4.1). A web platform will be developed to allow the management of the products including eligibility and prioritization criteria, monitoring, and reporting.

# Payments and Reimbursements

In general, the method of payment of CFN and/or CONAFIPS to CAF shall preferably be in the form of capital deposits, debt interest, with a maximum of one year’s responsibility. The terms of the loan will be established in accordance with CFN and/or CONAFIPS and the LFC’s policies.

# Guarantee

CAF requires a Security Guarantor of Ecuador’s Government. The guarantee granted by the National Treasury (NT) to CAF for the loans granted under the CAF-ECUARDO Program (LFCs) under the relevant Loan Agreement, in the same terms and conditions set forth herein.

# Terms of sub-loans and grace period for the LFCs

The terms of the sub-loans and grace period granted by CFN and/or CONAFIPS to the LFCs will be established in accordance with CFN and/or CONAFIPS policies. The interest rate charged on loans granted to the LFCs cannot be contrary to CFN and/or CONAFIPS policies. However, CFN and/or CONAFIPS may grant a grace period for the repayment of the loan.

# Interest Rate for CFN and CONAFIPS

The minimum interest rate applicable to the CFN and/or CONAFIPS is determined by CAF’s Finance Vice Presidency and approved by CFN and/or CONAFIPS’s President in view of Ecuador’s economic conditions, market conditions, and other relevant factors. The interest rate regulations, CAF sets depends directly on the Loan/Endorsement plus the rate CAF has established for the LFCs.

# Interest Rate for the LFC

The minimum interest rate applicable to the LFC is determined by CFN and/or CONAFIPS’s President in view of Ecuador’s economic conditions, market conditions. The interest rate regulations, CAF sets depends directly on the Loan/Endorsement plus the rate CAF has established for the LFCs.

# Prepayment

LFCs can voluntarily prepay the pending capital amount, either in full or partially, at any moment before the end of the term. This prepayment will be subject to CAF’s Policy and in agreement to provisions established in the signed F&A.

# Description of Program Components

- **Components and outcomes are structured in three components described in the following sections**.
  - For a detailed description, please refer to Annexes Section 2 and its appendices.
  
- The figure below aims to introduce to the logic of the intervention, as a continuation of the Theory of Change diagram presented in the previous section. The proportion of the loan and the grant proceeds from the GEF, the AEC and the beneficiaries, is shown from the perspective of the beneficiaries. This helps to understand how the components are interrelated and how they contribute to the achievement of the overall sustainability strategy will be to guarantee that the activities will continue to be funded.

- **Each component has a specific objective**:
  - Component (1): The LFCs will finance projects in the areas of rural tourism, agriculture, fishing; that will invest in different climate solutions that are organized in different components (e.g., a restaurant can be part of the tourism component and also part of the agricultural component).
  - Component (2): A farmer will be able to ask for a loan that includes resilient farming practices (Component 2) but also additional services such as training, technical assistance, access to markets, etc. This is an investment to ecosystems, capacity building, knowledge and awareness-raising, but also with strong support to the credit system.
  - Component (3): The LFCs will finance projects in the areas of rural tourism, agriculture, fishing; that will invest in different climate solutions that are organized in different components (e.g., a restaurant can be part of the tourism component and also part of the agricultural component).
  
- **The list of fully named activities that are connected to the diagram is presented as a way to guide the reader through the components**.

### Component 1: Energy matrix change in the Galápagos archipelago
- **Component 1** will reduce the energy dependency of the Galápagos livelihoods through increased low-emission energy access and power generation and will reduce the energy consumption by the Galápagos livelihoods through energy efficiency. The current energy matrix is based on fossil fuels (84% of total energy demand) with a high share of diesel (61%) and kerosene (23%). The remaining 13% comes from renewable energy sources such as solar, wind, and geothermal power plants. The Galápagos Energy Strategy (PEGSAG), 84% of the archipelago's energy matrix is based on fossil fuels with diesel: 16% on renewable energy...

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.38 | PAGE OF 152

## Outcome 1.1: Reduced energy dependency of the Galápagos livelihoods through increased low-emission energy access and power generation.

### Activity 1.1.1: Promote renewable energy generation and storage.
#### Activity 1.1.1.1: Promotion of centralized renewable energy generation.
- The Programme will finance the development of the Conolophus solar photovoltaic power generation project at the Galápagos National Park, which will be financed by a loan from the World Bank, to which CAF is HE for the activity and will be repaid in 20 years. Due to level of progress and its high impact, the Conolophus project has been prioritized over the other projects that are part of the PECGAS. The Conolophus project consists of a photovoltaic power generation system with an installed capacity of 15 MWp, which will be connected to the national grid via a 30-km transmission line and a 20-MW battery energy storage system, a switching station at 34.5 kV, 49 km of sub-transmission line, and a connection point in the Galápagos National Park. The Conolophus project will be connected to the national grid via a 138-kV transmission line and substation will be located in a WWF decommissioned runway in Baltra island, following the recommendation of the Galápagos National Park. The Conolophus project is expected to generate approximately 20 GWh per year, which represents 7% of the renewable energy penetration accounted for 14.4%. The PV Conolophus Project should provide the remaining amount needed to reach the target of 35% renewable energy penetration in Galápagos by 2030.
- Please refer to Annex 2 Section 12.4 for further details on the technical feasibility that includes description of the equipment, design and operation of the Conolophus project.

- This activity is expected to reduce an average of 18,859 tCO2e/year due to avoided diesel-based generation and aims at reducing the dependency of Galápagos on fossil fuels for electricity production.
- The project model refers to a public-private partnership where the private organization builds, owns, operates, and transfers the facilities after a 25-year concession period. Further details on contractual and legal aspects are described in Annex 3 Section 14.

#### Activity 1.1.1.2: Distributed renewable power generation projects.
- An intermediation scheme will allow providing banks to invest in Micro distributed PV generation through the Galápagos Energy Fund (GEF). The GEF is a financial vehicle that will be managed by the CAF, CONAPRIS through local banks present in Galápagos. The tourism sector will be particularly targeted due to the strong potential of this sector for renewable energy and its high level of consumption of electricity in all sectors.
- Nevertheless, beneficiaries of the commercial, educational and sport centers, office, residential and private buildings are also eligible as long as they meet the eligibility conditions described below. The GEF will focus on the sectors with the largest energy consumption: Floreana island was not considered because it does not have a significant tourism sector and therefore has low electricity demand.
- The eligibility conditions for accessing credit for distributed RE generation are listed in the table below.

| Conditions | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Beneficiaries | Ecotourism value chain such as hotels, restaurants, and boat operators. Commercial buildings (e.g., shops, offices, etc.). Educational institutions (schools, colleges, etc.) |
| Eligible investments | Small scale solar PV systems |
| Minimum objectives | 80% reduced Energy consumption |
| Financing range | $500 to $40,000 |
| Minimum co-financing by the beneficiary | 20% |
| EAS category | B or C (category A not an eligible) |
| Other conditions | Micro distributed PV generation must comply with the current regulation on equipment specifications and protection requirements. |

## Output 1: Strengthening Executing Entities and stakeholders' capacities for the development and implementation of mitigation projects

### Activity 1.2.2.1: Technical Assistance facility for energy investments

#### Matchmaking events to facilitate financing of mitigation projects
- These events are aimed at building a bridge between renewable energy technology service providers, local banks and stakeholders in the tourism, agricultural and artisanal fishing sectors.
- Matchmaking events will be held based on the criteria set by the service providers to facilitate project development.

### Technical Assistance for Public Local Banks
- Aimed at public local banks that grant loans under the Programme to strengthen the capacity of officers, executives and staff involved in the evaluation of energy projects.
- Training will include training on eligibility criteria, and determine potential bankable projects. Training will be offered about RE and EE project evaluations, including financial analysis, environmental and social risk management of projects: gender perspective, and definition of GHG baseline and MWh.

### Component 2: Building climate resilience of the Galapagos livelihoods
- This component will enhance the climate resilience of the Galapagos' livelihoods by strengthening the food system and improving access to water.
- Activities will be executed in funding by FAO, WFP, and CAF or CONAPARIS will be the EE for providing funds for activities under Outputs 2.1.2 and 2.1.5.

### Outcome 2.1: Galapagos food system is climate resilient for both internal consumption and for the sustainable tourism sector

## B

### Main objectives of the project:
- Strengthening the islands' food system in the face of climate change, resulting in a resilience net for the Carriacou's agriculture and small-scale fisheries livelihoods through increased availability of local food to supply resident and visitor populations. The activities under this outcome have also mitigation co-benefits.

### Outputs:
1. **Output 2.1.1: Enhanced institutional capacity for climate-resilient planning and development**
   - Activities under Output 2.1.1 will be financed through the credit line.
   - Eligibility conditions for accessing loans for investments in sustainable land use.

#### Table 7. Eligibility for accessing loans for investments in sustainable land use

| Condition | Description |
|-----------|-------------|
| Beneficiaries | Farmers (individuals, cooperatives, associations, MSMEs) |
| Eligible investments | Smallholder systems, Situational analysis, Climate-smart agriculture equipment, working capital (seed, feed to start the process), and inputs to adapt to changes in water availability, soil fertility, and temperature increase. |
| Eligibility of beneficiaries | A (C category is not eligible). |
| Minimum objectives | Participation on farming field schools. |
| Financing thresholds | From USD 50,000 to USD 100,000. |
| Maximum amount per beneficiary | B or C (category A is not eligible) |
| Other conditions | The project will provide a technical assistance activities and capacity building process will be favored. No purchase of additional livestock, or the expansion of the agricultural frontier. |

#### Output 2.1.1: Enhanced institutional capacity for climate-resilient planning and development
- Activities proposed in this output will improve the knowledge of Carriacou Government staff and vulnerable farmers about climate change and its impacts on their livelihoods, location, and adaptation to changes in water availability.
- The generation and access to hydro-meteorological information for decision-making in a changing climate will be strengthened, and consensus-building processes will be facilitated. This will ensure that the adaptive capacity of farmers and allow climate change adaptation planning to be sustained beyond the activities proposed under this output.

#### Activity 2.1.1.2: Implement a hydro-meteorological monitoring system to inform and tailor the information to the needs of vulnerable smallholder farmers
- This activity will develop a capacity building program throughout the 5 years of Program implementation to provide Carriacou Government staff, including those from the National Agricultural Research Institute (NAR), and the Galagala Council Government (CGGEG) with technical knowledge, so they are capable of implementing a hydro-meteorological monitoring system. The system will be based on climate change adaptation and mitigation practices based on local knowledge and conditions to guarantee sustainability of the activities of this program beyond its implementation period.


---
**Page 35**
---

Access, generation, and delivery information is critical in the process of enhancing the adaptive capacities of rural areas to climate change. This action will collect data, produce information, analysis, and interpret, and disseminate information for decision making at different levels, and for the farmers. This activity looks to address the existing gaps in the monitoring system by providing a more comprehensive approach that includes both government agencies and scientific organizations to provide “in-time” information and services, which will allow the communities to adapt their agricultural practices to climate change. The proposed integrated rural monitoring system will be procured by FAD and will be implemented by the Galapagos Science Center (GSC) in collaboration with INAMHI. The GSC will provide technical assistance for the implementation of this project and the equipment and hydrometeorological stations will form part of the INAMHI and this organization together with the GCS will establish a database to store all information.

The monitoring system includes: a) Climatological monitoring, b) Surface hydrology monitoring, c) Groundwater monitoring, d) Soil moisture monitoring, e) Water quality monitoring. The GSC will provide technical assistance for the aggregate and distribute this information. Technical staff will be trained in the use of equipment, process of data, hydrometeorological stations.

This activity will be implemented throughout Years 1 to 5 and will provide infrastructure to collect groundwater, manage the surplus and collect surface water data to cover 37 km2 in Santa Cruz, 32 km2 in San Cristobal and 17 km2 in Floreana. The project will also include the installation of 40 rain gauges in the communities of 8 Municipalities, Government agencies: INAMHI, UNOPS, CIGRED, NIMAHU, Universities and NGOs. Indirect beneficiaries are the local population.

Output 2.1.2 Increased number of farmers reached with climate-resilient water and agricultural food production technologies

The activities of this output seek to transform degraded agricultural areas into healthy agroecosystems to enhance climate change adaptation capacities, optimizing quality in all aspects of agriculture and the environment, by respecting the natural balance of ecosystems and the biodiversity of the Galapagos Islands and other archipelago islands. These activities will also lead to improved water recharge and productivity and contribute to the population’s food security. The project will provide training for farmers on climate change adaptation practices that increase availability of water for agriculture, especially in dry seasons; one of the activities will help better access storage and management of water resources through the construction of small reservoirs and rainwater harvesting systems and loans will be facilitated to farmers through the Galapagos Climate Credit Line (GCCCL) according to the eligibility criteria. The project will also provide training on climate change adaptation practices, including agroecological practices and technologies that help to manage soils, water, and biomass to enhance soil moisture/texture suitability for production of crops and livestock in a sustainable manner. These practices are based on agroecology principles and they are considered “non-regret” practices, considering climate change impacts.

Eligibility: the beneficiaries will be chosen by combining data from the rural cadastre and participatory rural appraisal (PRA) surveys. The project will provide training to farmers in the use of these technologies that have been estimated in such a way that 100% of the farms suitable for an activity will be able to carry it out. In all cases, in addition to the beneficiaries, the project will also train and provide technical assistance to the local Action Plan (30%).

2.1.2.1 Develop a physical and knowledge network for conservation and use of phylogenetic resources through in-situ and ex situ conservation activities.

This activity will allow access to quality seeds in sufficient quantity, as a decisive means of production to increase productivity and adaptability of crops to climate change. The project will provide training on the importance of their bargaining power in the local agro-food chain through improved access to adapted seeds to environmental changes.

Exchanges will be promoted with the International Maize and Wheat Improvement Center (CIMMYT) of seeds that are adapted to climate change conditions, particularly on drought-resilient genetic materials that have been already selected. The project will also provide training for farmers in the use of these technologies. With the adoption of adapted seeds, this project recovers and promotes the use of existing cultivars resistant to different biotic changes generated by climate change such as pests, diseases, weeds, and droughts. Through providing shaded areas and shelter, the overall climate variability is reduced, and therefore the risk of crop failure is minimized. The project will also provide training on the importance of transpiration, and increase soil fertility. With the support of extension services from the INAMPH, farmers will explore, find, select, and conserve seeds in their own fields and communities to be used as a source for future quality seed banks. The following sub-activities will allow to improve the adaptive capacity of the agroecosystems

Galapagos will implement in-farm conservation activities: collect, conserve, use and distribute the agrobiodiversity existing in Galapagos (community-based seed bank), with special focus on the variety of crops resistant to biotic challenges and climate change. The project will also develop a seed multiplication system that will work as an agrobiodiversity repository, knowledge center and distribution facility, for long-term conservation.

### Targets: 
The direct beneficiaries are INAP seed banks, and 25 "seed" farmers distributed in the four islands. In addition, 100 farmers will be trained to produce seeds of climate-resilient crops, which will be efficient production and distribution centers for the Galapagos agrobiodiversity repository.

### Project Activities:
The project activities will be implemented over Years 2 to 4 in ESM (Environmental Services Module), with a focus on conservation, seed multiplication, and mixed production. Indirect beneficiaries: 755 farm households and 19,000 hectares. 6 locations (100% of farms).

#### Activity 2.1.2.2. Implement an integrated climate-resilient crop management system at farm level:
- This activity will implement a mixed cropping system with organic fertilizers to reduce soil erosion, droughts and the attacks of pests driven by climate change. As part of the agroecological approach, the Integrated Crop Management (ICM) will be implemented in all farms. ICM is an integrated approach that combines conventional and climate change climate. ICM will be incorporated into daily management of the production system, through technical assistance to farmers and training sessions on climate-resilient crop management systems.

#### Targets: 
This activity will be implemented in years 1 to 4 at least 50% of the total Galapagos farms (404 farms), distributed in the following way: a. All farm ICM practices will be implemented in medium and small-scale farms, covering 300 farms (75%). b. At least 20% of the total Galapagos farms will be converted to large-scale farms, covering 10 farms (210 beneficiaries; 30%), excluding livestock production.

### Activity 2.1.2.3. Implement a silvopastoral system in Galapagos for cattle ranching:
- This activity will implement a silvopastoral system (SPS) in Galapagos for cattle ranching to improve productivity and to integrate the management of the invasive species Posidonia galapagensis (grass) and endemism species. The SPS will be implemented on 100 farms, covering 25% of all livestock production units. This system generates a share of ecological services and reduces guano deposition in cattle ranches.
- Farmers will receive technical assistance from the Galapagos National Park Service (GNP) and the Galapagos Conservation Trust (GCT), as well as training sessions on climate-resilient crop management systems, with the perspective of Forest Management with Integrated Livestock, as a possibility of restoring productive livestock activities in Galapagos. The SPS will be implemented to reduce the vulnerability of livestock production to climate change as they stabilize forage availability throughout the year and increase biodiversity.

#### GHG mitigation co-benefit:
- GHG mitigation co-benefit will be derived not only from the carbon sequestration of the introduction of trees and bushes, but also by reducing the need for synthetically produced farm inputs and from reducing the need to expand agricultural land.

### Agroecological SPS is being implemented in 244 farms in medium and large-scale farms mostly devoted for cattle production. The main crops are: Livestock (dry-milked cows, dairy cows), banana, pineapple, papaya, avocado, and paddock diversion. All these activities are on farms with over 20 cows (68 farms). Additionally, 36 livestock production farms will be implemented by biodigesters: 42 in cattle production farms and 24 in swine production farms.

#### Activity 2.1.2.4. Develop and implement water collection and water management systems for climate-resilient food production:
- Water scarcity is the major problem in the Galapagos agroecosystems, which is mainly caused by changing rainfall patterns and higher temperatures. The lack of rain and scarce water available in the islands have even prompted authorities to declare a state of emergency in 2015. This activity will implement an efficient irrigation system for the agricultural sector in the Galapagos Islands by including 300 new hectares with climate-resilient farming systems, which will be implemented using the most efficient water irrigation system aimed at increasing its diversity and redundancy both in sources and in operating.

### Output 2.1.3.2 Adaptive co-management of the Galápagos marine zoning through integrated science-based and participatory processes

- To prevent and mitigate the impact of the ENSO and climate change on marine ecosystems such as sea level rise, coral bleaching, and fishery resources.
- The adaptive co-management process will be based on scientific evidence of the impact of climate change on fishery resources, marine biodiversity, and fisheries' livelihoods.

### One of the main barriers for an effective implementation of the current marine zoning has been the lack of scientific evidence about its impacts. This is due to a lack of knowledge about the effects of human activities (e.g., fishing) in Galápagos waters and the need to generate new scientific knowledge about the impact of the former Galápagos marine zoning, and about the effects of human and climate change on fishery resources, marine biodiversity, and fisheries' livelihoods. To address this barrier, a set of research questions will be generated (see Table 2.1.3.2.1). Based on this knowledge, the GNPDG and Galápagos Governing Council will receive a set of recommendations to improve the adaptive co-management process for the new marine zoning.

### One of the most important challenges for the effective implementation of the new marine zoning is to re-establish the participatory consultation process that was used in the former marine zoning. The GNPDG and Galápagos Governing Council will engage with stakeholders in the re-zoning process by engaging extensive and participatory consultation in the CEF. This will ensure that all relevant stakeholders are involved, including local communities who have a stake in the new marine zoning since it will provide a voice to several members of local coastal communities who have influence or are influential in the decision-making processes.

### Activity 2.1.3.2 Design and implement an advanced data system for the adaptive co-management of the Galápagos marine zoning

- The adaptive co-management of the Galápagos marine zoning requires better data collection, dimensioned to inform appropriate indicators in a faster and more accessible format for reporting, processing, and analysis that will translate into improved decision-making.
- The utilization of high-tech advanced data systems has been precluded by limitations of funding and institutional infrastructure. To address this barrier, an app, a data repository, and a decision-support system will be developed to improve the adaptive co-management process.

### A decision-support system, called the "Sustainable Ecological Monitoring" module, will be created following the transdisciplinary methodology recommended by Bradley et al. (2019). This module will be developed in collaboration with the

**GFMR and GGRs are fully integrated into the "Consolidated Environmental Information System (Sistema Unico de Información Ambiental - SUIA)," which is the national data repository system for environmental data in Ecuador.**

### Action 2.1.1.3: Transitioning from the current decision-making framework to inform the adaptive co-management of the Galapagos Marine Reserve.

*The effective adaptive management of the GMR requires a structured decision-making framework linked to the development and implementation of an adaptive management plan for the GMR, which will be developed in close collaboration with the GFND and Charles Darwin Foundation, which are fundamental to improve the management effectiveness of the GMR. The proposed approach is based on the following elements: (1) developing a structured decision-making model that involves: (i) refining management objectives and modeling ecosystem behavior; (ii) identifying key drivers of change in the marine environment and their impacts on the GMR, including climate change options.

*For the effective implementation of this structured decision-making framework, the project will invest in the development of a training program for local management authorities, scientists, NGO, and relevant stakeholders. This training will be based on the integration of the structured decision-making framework into GFMR decision-making process and existing monitoring programs.*

Moreover, a cooperation agreement will be sought to align the priorities of the Galapagos Marine Reserve Fund (GMRF) with those of the project (as defined in the attached annexes to this proposal), with the activities proposed in this Program, and therefore foster the channeling of GFMS funds to the GMR.

### Output 2.1.4: Increased adoption of climate-smart small-scale fisheries and aquaculture approaches.

*Alternative fisheries management measures are required to reduce the impact of tuna fisheries and ensure the resilience of Galapagos marine ecosystems in a changing environment, including through better livelihoods, contributing to mitigating the impact of the ENSO and climate changes. Activities under this Output will be executed by WFN and GFND.*

#### Activity 2.1.4.1: Management conditions of small scale tuna fisheries, strengthening to reduce the ecological impact of the fishery over time and improve the sustainability of species.

*The Project will build on the alliance created by 12 governmental and non-governmental organizations in 2019; to maximize the economic value of the Galapagos tuna fishery and minimize its ecological impact by improving its management. The project will implement a series of actions that will contribute to the development of a new model that takes place inside and outside the boundaries of the GMR, taking into consideration the impacts of climate change on fisheries, including overfishing, bottom trawling and foreign fishing; determine the migratory patterns and the general population structure of yellowfin tuna from the GMR.*

#### Activity 2.1.4.2: Implementation of a new model for tuna fishery management.

*Specifically, this activity will implement an electronic monitoring system that allows the cost-efficient collection of data on tuna fishing activities in the Galapagos Islands and surrounding waters; develop a new model to improve post-harvest handling and cold chain infrastructure; design and implement a code of good fishing practices with a specific focus on tuna fisheries. The project will also support the development of a new monitoring system, research priorities identified by the GFND and stakeholders to improve the management and sustainability of Galapagos tuna fisheries. This will include: (1) developing an electronic monitoring system for tuna fishing that takes place inside and outside the boundaries of the GMR; taking into consideration the impacts of climate change on fisheries, including overfishing, bottom trawling and foreign fishing; determine the migratory patterns and the general population structure of yellowfin tuna from the GMR.

# Activity 2.1.3.4 Small-scale aquaculture and experimental allocation of Territorial Use Rights for Fishing (TURFs) combined with marine protected areas

This activity will support the development of small-scale aquaculture in Galapagos, in combination with the experimental allocation of Territorial Use Rights for Fishing (TURFs), to turbin sea cucumbers and, to provide an alternative livelihood for vulnerable groups and families.

The project will be implemented by the University of the Andes and Universidad de las Américas y Tecnologías de Manabí.

* The population of sea cucumbers has been substantially below carrying capacity as a result of overfishing. Restocking may be the only active management intervention that can boost population recovery. Restocking or reseeding is not an option for this species because it requires a very large number of individuals to be released, which would be achieved by natural recovery (Lorentzen et al., 2013). To this end, and a substantial number of sea cucumbers will be cultivated in captivity. The project will also include the development of an experimental reseeding.
* This management approach will maximize the contribution of wild and released cultured sea cucumber to population recovery, while minimizing the risk that the introduced individuals may have negative impacts on native populations and developmental manipulations likewise may be carried out to produce "wildlife" sea cucumbers.

The specific activities that will be carried out are:
1. Update stock assessment of L. turbinis, including projections for future trends in population size.
2. Develop a protocol for the cultivation of sea cucumbers in captivity and release them into the wild.
3. Experimental allocation of TURFs to fishermen who have been identified as potential beneficiaries of this activity.

The project will be implemented by the Universidad de las Américas y Tecnologías de Manabí, with the support of the Galapagos National Park (GNP) and the Galapagos Conservation Trust (GCT). The GNP will be responsible for the administration of the TURFs and will be in charge of entering the TURFs through the vessel monitoring system (VMS) and Automatic Identification System (AIS), which will allow the GNP to monitor fishing activities within the TURFs. The GCT will provide the funding necessary to carry out this activity, and will also grant the TURFs.

Output 2.1.3.4.1: More sustainable and more efficient value chain for climate-smart seafood and agricultural products, with links to new markets.

## Eligibility: as in the case of the activities under Output 2.1.2, the beneficiaries will be chosen by combining data from the quantitative and participatory rural appraisals, which will make it possible to define the activities that are appropriate for each farm.

The budget has been designed in such a way that 10% of the funds suitable for an activity under Output 2.1.2 will be allocated to this activity. The remaining 90% of the budget will be earmarked with the minimum established in the Gender Action Plan (30%).

### Activity 2.1.2.4: Promotion of a circular economy through new value-added and socially responsible export enterprises

This activity will strengthen the traceability of dairy and meat products in Galapagos food systems to improve their positioning on the local market and increase their profitability at all stages of the value chain. This activity will:

- At the production stage: Strengthen the capacity for sustainable livestock production, with a focus on resilience:
  - At the processing stage: strengthen processes of manufacturing and adding value to products and packaging them in a way that is attractive to consumers.
  - At the Market stage: improve viability in food availability and quality and establish local systems for the fair trade of Galapagos products.

The beneficiaries will be the same as those where silvopastoral practices will be implemented. 244 farms in medium and large-scale livestock production will benefit from this activity.

### Mitigation co-benefits will come from:

- The conservation and efficient use of natural resources.
- A shift away from extensive livestock production strategies with high GHG emissions, in favor of intensive and semi-intensive livestock production systems that are more sustainable and have lower environmental impacts. This will be achieved by improving the efficiency of the manufacturing and processing methods for meat and dairy products with resource-efficient technologies.

### Activity 2.1.2.5: Strengthening the capacity for sustainable coffee production

The 2014 Census of Agricultural Production Units suggests that coffee growing is the agricultural activity with highest income generation for farmers. Coffee production generates a cumulative gross annual income of USD 683,974 (USD 2,815 per farm) and employs an average of 1.5 people per farm (ranging from 0 to 10). There are 1,000 hectares and 63 farms (3,556 hectares) of coffee plantations mixed with other crops, distributed across three oriental provinces: San Cristóbal, Santa Cruz, and Santiago.

The objective is to improve the quality of coffee production by:

- Covering the surface of Galapagos agroforestry systems with quality coffee plants.
- Maintaining and increasing the use of organic fertilizers and avoiding the use of chemical fertilizers that can cause soil acidification and deterioration.
- Coffee plants improve soil structure through roots growth and by adding to the leaf litter. Coffee also establishes a natural barrier against wind erosion, which is very important in Galapagos due to its high winds and humidity with their changes. Furthermore, active coffee plantations prevent the expansion of invasive plant species.

Sub-activities include enhancing coffee harvesting, mobilizing production to the local coffee agro-processing plants, and strengthening the capacity for sustainable coffee production through a training center. This action will include the 20 farms that exclusively produce coffee and 30 farms with mixed crops.

### Activity 2.1.2.6: Strengthening the capacity of micro-enterprises

This activity will develop micro-enterprises that add value to potential agricultural products (bananas, plantains, cassava, citrus, medicinal herbs, vegetables) from integrated production systems, in which the medium term contribution is to increase their income and improve their quality of life. The strengthening of small-scale agroforestry really represents the most efficient and easy way to achieve climate resilience of agriculture with reduced costs.

The actions proposed include strengthening the knowledge and norms for processing these products, constructing two agro-processing plants, and building public policies to position them in the market.

### Activity 2.1.2.7: Strengthening the capacity for sustainable production of fruits

This activity will strengthen the capacity for sustainable production of fruits (bananas, plantains, Casu Fruits and chics, ago-processing of preserves and pulps of citrus fruits, pineapple and tomato and agro-processing of aromatic and medicinal herbs).

By supplying the agro-processing plants, 408 farms (346 agricultural and 62 integral) will be direct beneficiaries. It is expected that the income generated by the processing plant will be within the framework of the Social and Solidarity Economy and Fair Trade.

### Activity 2.1.2.8: Promotion of a circular economy through new value-added and socially responsible export enterprises

Previous projects have failed in their effort to improve the management and marketing system of Galapagos small-scale producers, due to the lack of technical assistance, the absence of market information, the low level of organization, and without adequate and sustained institutional and financial support to ensure the creation of the

### Necessary conditions to take advantage of the business opportunities offered by the Galapagos small-scale fisheries.

- **Through this activity, the program will create** the "Galapagos Virtual Innovation Lab" (G-ViLab), an inter-institutional and interdisciplinary platform that integrates and coordinates the government's existing programs and projects for the promotion and development of sustainable fisheries. The main objective of the G-ViLab is to provide technical assistance to local fishers and entrepreneurs in Galapagos, including small-scale fishing cooperatives, seafood companies, and civil society entrepreneurs, in aspects related to social innovation for climate resilience, sustainability, and competitiveness.

- **Based on the results** of the implementation of the G-ViLab, technical assistance is to be provided to local fishers and entrepreneurs in Galapagos. This assistance will be provided through a soft credit line that will be established for entrepreneurs within the Galapagos Climate Credit Line (GCCl), to foster the financial inclusion of fishers and entrepreneurs from civil society interested in investing in sustainable fisheries practices.

- **One of the main challenges** is to improve the productivity, sustainability, and competitiveness of Galapagos small-scale fishing. To address this challenge, a soft credit line will be established for entrepreneurs within the GCCl, to those companies or individuals that show an attractive investment plan, which would be developed with technical assistance from the G-ViLab.

### The eligibility conditions for accessing loans for sustainable fishing practices are described in the table below:

| Condition | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Beneficiaries | Fishers and entrepreneurs from civil society willing to adopt sustainable fishing practices, in exchange for reduced financial support on the use of seabirds based on common property rights. |
| Eligible investments | To the extent feasible, business plans for all seafood ventures should incorporate and demonstrate their alignment with three principles: |
| - | * Strong value proposition for investment under prospective pilot operations. |
| - | * Strong economic viability and profitability of fisheries, particularly a strong value proposition for investment under prospective pilot operations. |
| - | * Effective distribution of wealth from enhanced fisheries, including income and livelihood diversification, recognizing the key role of women in fisheries value chains. |
| - | * Biodiversity conservation and ecosystem health: reduce the ecological impact of fishing activities on marine biodiversity by managing fishing pressure over commercialized and protected species. |

- **Minimum co-financing required for the project**: From USD 10,000 to USD 50,000
