### Overview of Marine High Ecological Value Areas (HEVA)

#### Objectives:
- Increase awareness and understanding of HEVAs in the GMR under restoration schemes taking into account potential climate change scenarios.
- Activities will be executed by WWF with GCF non-reimbursable resources.

#### Key Activities:

1. **Invasion Science and Management Solutions:**
   - Mobilize and prioritize invasive species management solutions to prevent, reduce, and reverse the expected impacts of marine invasive species related to climate change scenarios.
   - Invasive species globally produce damages estimated at more than 5% of global GDP; invasive species have already been introduced into the GMR through ballast water, hull fouling, and other means. The introduction of non-native species has significant impacts on biodiversity. Preventing the introduction of new alien species through biosecurity is the most cost-effective approach to reduce these risks.

2. **Current Biosecurity Actions:**
   - Currently, biosecurity actions are led by the Agency for Regulation and Control of Biosecurity and Quarantine (ARCOBAC) in collaboration with WWF. These efforts focus on marine NIS being introduced through marine traffic and not natural marine dispersal.
   - It is necessary to strengthen these efforts by increasing the number of trained personnel, improving equipment, and providing training and equipment. These are urgently required to minimize the risk of introduction of marine NIS that have the potential for significant impacts in the GMR.

3. **Activity 2.1.1 Strengthening Marine Biosecurity:**
   - This activity will address a major driver in biodiversity loss by (a) creating risk analysis and ranking systems for biosecurity, (b) creating effective Early Detection and Rapid Response (EDRR) protocols to diminish new NIS invasions, and (c) implementing an outreach campaign to raise awareness of the risks associated with marine invasive species in the GMR. This will allow for rapid detection of threats, improve coordination between local stakeholders and authorities, and provide a framework for collaboration among key actors such as government agencies and citizen science.
   - Complementarily, the program will implement a regional outreach campaign to showcase and promote best practices in marine biosecurity and EDRR.

4. **Activity 2.1.2 Restore Coral Reef Ecosystems:**
   - This activity will restore coral reef ecosystems and strengthen the controls of biopiracy and coral bleaching in critical High-Ecological Value Areas (HEVAs) of the GMR.
   - Corals in Galapagos have suffered (85% of damage) due to bleaching events as a result from the increased sea surface temperature (SST) recorded between 1982-1993 and 1997-1998. Most coral species present within the GMR, such as Porites lobata and Pavona clavus, are highly sensitive to bleaching events.
   - SST in the GMR has a mean temperature of 24°C with an annual range between 20°C and 30°C. The projected increase in SST by 2100 will have significant impacts on coral reefs (Pattan et al., 2015). Future climate scenarios project RCP 4.5 and 8.5 for the year 2090 to predict a mean sea surface temperature of 30°C, which is expected to cause widespread bleaching events in the GMR.
   - Further, sea temperatures with the GMR reached 30°C in April 2013 under RCP 8.5, and the projections for RCP 4.5 by the end of this century are also projected at 30°C (Banks et al., 2016). Under these conditions, additional, changing current systems can also have large impacts upon the ecology of a marine system such as closing off access to breeding grounds and reducing connectivity between islands.
   - The development of coral reefs in Galapagos is highly dependent on larval dispersal. At a depth of 5 m for both shallow and deep water corals, there are no significant currents that allow for further visualization of the connectivity between islands due to larval dispersal. At a depth of 5 m for both shallow and deep water corals, there are no significant currents that allow for further visualization of the connectivity between islands due to larval dispersal.
   - This is likely due to increased current magnitude which drugs the larvae out of the reserve into the open ocean where they are unable to find suitable habitat. The development of coral reefs in Galapagos is highly dependent on larval dispersal, and this is likely due to increased current magnitude which drugs the larvae out of the reserve into the open ocean where they are unable to find suitable habitat.
   - This activity will address a major driver in biodiversity loss by (a) creating risk analysis and ranking systems for biosecurity, (b) creating effective Early Detection and Rapid Response (EDRR) protocols to diminish new NIS invasions, and (c) implementing an outreach campaign to raise awareness of the risks associated with marine invasive species in the GMR. This will allow for rapid detection of threats, improve coordination between local stakeholders and authorities, and provide a framework for collaboration among key actors such as government agencies and citizen science.
   - Complementarily, the program will implement a regional outreach campaign to showcase and promote best practices in marine biosecurity and EDRR.

#### References:
- Banks et al., 2016
- Pattan et al., 2015

At the same time, it has been discovered that among coral communities, there are some species that show a much higher resistance to certain climate change and bleaching (Hughes et al., 2018; Oliver and Palumbi, 2017). This resilience enables communities to withstand disturbances and regain their functions and dominance in the environment. The project will use this knowledge to determine which coral species are most resilient and best suited for determining the feasibility of planting coral and conduct experiments to increase survival and reduce mortality rates. Different environmental conditions such as water temperature, salinity, light intensity, and nutrient availability can affect how well corals grow. And temperature and light can determine the makeup of the initial colonizers while sediment that accumulates on the reef surface can also influence which species are able to settle.

### Activity 2.2.1 Improve surveillance and control measures for adequate sea turtle nesting and foraging in the Galapagos to counteract potential effects of climate change on their reproductive success.

- Climate change is expected to have a significant impact on the terrestrial reproductive phase, which is only successful within a limited range of environmental and physical conditions. Expected increases in temperature may cause nest mortality (Fuentes et al., 2014; Hamann et al., 2015) and increase the risk of flooding (Limpens et al., 2009, 2010). These conditions are likely to become less optimal as climate change progresses. Besides, nesting habitat loss is expected due to sea level rise and increased coastal erosion (Fuentes et al., 2014; Hamann et al., 2015), which may cause egg mortality and loss and/or abandonment of nesting beaches (Fish et al., 2008). As nesting areas disappear, turtles will be forced to nest in less optimal conditions. This is already happening on some islands (Gironardi et al., 2003; Twain et al., 2006). Thus, sea turtle adaptation depends at least in part on the effectiveness and adaptability of their foraging strategies. Sea turtles are known to be highly adaptable to changes in environmental conditions, including anthropogenic stress (Hamann et al., 2015). Following the recommendations proposed by Fuentes et al. (2014), specific measures should be taken to protect nesting beaches from erosion and flooding, as well as to shift from habitat protection to more active and direct manipulation of nests and the nesting habitats together with their critical foraging areas.

### This activity, funded by the GCF grant executed by WWF, proposes to tackle effects of climate change by (1) directly manipulate turtle's nests to protect them from the direct impacts of erosion and flooding at most important nesting beaches; (2) monitor sea turtles' movements in relation to their critical foraging areas during natural land strikes; and (3) monitor and assess key habitats within the GMR to assess the success of the measures applied.

### The activity will ensure that sea turtles remain in their role of maintaining the habitat for fisheries species that are crucial for local communities. It also aims at ensuring the conservation of marine biodiversity, including sea turtles during species-recreational diving activities within the GMR and therefore its maintenance is essential for Galapagos' tourism industry.

* **This activity**, funded by the GCF grant executed by WWF, seeks to increase the resilience of rain-forest ecosystems through restoration and restoration approaches, while strengthening the ongoing invasive species control program set up by the GNP. This proposal focuses on the landscapes of the highlands of Santa Cruz, San Carlos, and San Ignacio de Velasco in Bolivia. The project area is 450,000 ha, with the GNP itself hosting the last remnant of Scalesia forests. Overall, the implementation area covers approximately 45,000 ha within an elevation range from 120 to 3800 m.

Please refer to Annex II – Section 12.2.3 for further details on the areas included, the ecosystem services they provide, and the restoration approaches that will be implemented in each area.

### Activity 2.2.1 Strengthen control programs for invasive plant species, especially blackberry, in protected agricultural areas, based on projected dynamics of their expansion under climate change scenarios

This activity aims to limit the impact of invasive species, the expression of which can be exacerbated by climate change:

* **Invasive species:** The main focus is on *Rubus fruticosus* (blackberry) and *Lantana camara*, with the distribution and therefore impacts of invasive species on native and endemic species (cf. 3). Preserving remnant forest habitats from further invasion by these species will be a priority, as well as preventing new invasions in areas where they are not yet present but have high ecological importance. If invasive plant species control is proposed see Annex II appendix 2. The sites considered for the implementation of this activity include: (1) the conservation and restoration of Scalesia forests; (2) the conservation and restoration of key remnant forest fragments within the Gargantua National Park; and (3) areas where blackberry and guava are being cultivated.

On the one hand, the control programs for invasive plant species will be strengthened in areas within the Gargantua National Park. Within this area, the following actions will be proposed: 1) verifying and adjusting guava and blackberry climate change scenarios; 2) identifying areas where blackberry and guava can be contained; and 3) aligning the Terrestrial Invasive Species Program of the GNP with long-term control under climate change scenarios.

On the other hand, the conservation programs for invasive plant species will also be implemented in farms. Specific actions include: 1) assessing the conservation status of 750 ha of Scalesia forest remnants in the agricultural areas; 2) promoting agroforestry practices (such as intercropping and mixed farming); 3) promoting the use of alternative agricultural practices (such as improvement of cattle grazing and agroforestry systems management); 4) to combat the spread of invasive species, such as blackberry and guava, in farms; and 5) promoting new land-use strategies that help preserve and promote native biodiversity while at the same time keeping the economic viability of the farm.

### Activity 2.2.2 Restore key remnant forest fragments inside and outside the GNP to enhance ecosystem services capacity and promote restoration

This will allow us to conserve and connect key remnant forest fragments on farms and in GNP areas and raise awareness of the importance of ecosystem services. This activity will be developed along three main lines:

* On the one hand, the restoration of key remnant forest fragments within the Gargantua National Park will be carried out by strengthening the conservation efforts already underway (cf. 3). The aim is to increase the number of seedlings and native species planted in key remnant forest fragments, as well as increasing the area of these fragments.

* On the other hand, the conservation and restoration of key remnant forest fragments on farms will be promoted. This includes: 1) promoting the planting of Scalesia trees and other native tree species; 2) strengthening the conservation efforts already underway (cf. 3); 3) increasing the number of seedlings and native species planted in key remnant forest fragments, and restoring 750 ha of farmland with Scalesia spp. and other native tree species with a total of 300,000 trees; and 4) promoting agroforestry practices.

* Finally, outreach activities and workshops will be conducted with the local community on the importance of ecosystem services and how they benefit livelihoods. Workshops and meetings will be held with farmers and local authorities to promote the planting of Scalesia spp., other native tree species, and agroforestry systems. Information materials will also be provided to show the progress of restoration activities.

### Activity 2.2.3 Implement pilot projects for the characterization and monitoring of forest restoration processes

Predicting future ecosystem dynamics depends critically on an improved understanding of how disturbances and climate change have driven long-term ecological changes in the past. Permanent plots allow for the characterization and monitoring of forest restoration processes, including the effects of different types of disturbance (e.g., pathogens, insects, windstorms, etc.). The pilots provide the context to analyze how these climate and human-driven processes are changing vegetation communities and ecosystem dynamics. Long term data from permanent plots

## Objective 3.1 Strengthened response capacity of key institutions, local livelihoods, and population from Galapagos islands to climate change

### Output 3.1.1 Tools and financial mechanisms established for the sustainability of the programme's actions.

#### Activity 3.1.1.1 Implementation on ecosystem certification scheme to adopt best practices across the tourism value chain.
- An action will be carried out by the GCF with GFN to establish a set of standards for climate resilient development for the Galapagos tourism sector, based on climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies. The activity aims at overcoming the obstacles faced by the tourism sector related to the current lack of knowledge about climate change and its impacts in the Galapagos islands' tourism destination.
- This activity aims to overcome the obstacles faced by the tourism sector related to the current lack of knowledge about climate change and its impacts in the Galapagos islands' tourism destination. It will also support the development of a certification scheme for the tourism sector as small and local family businesses that are suffering from a major economic crisis as a result of the COVID-19 pandemic, and which have not been able to access the financial resources required by the GNPV. However, these practices do not include measures with a climate perspective, e.g., low-emission transport.

#### Two key aspects of the certification proposal are:
* i) It will be sought to be managed by the local authorities and thus it is expected to offer much lower costs to the tourism actors.
* ii) Technical assistance will include supporting the business community in developing their own climate action plans.

- The activity will begin by identifying and training local auditors on the concepts, standards, and procedures for ecosystem certification. This will be followed by a series of workshops with key stakeholders from prioritized companies. This pilot will offer:
  * i) Audits: As an assistance plan to incorporate improvements and consolidate the implementation of climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies in the tourism sector.
  * ii) Evaluation of results to determine whether the objectives established in the proposed pilot were achieved.

- Finally, a final design of the certification program will be achieved, which will establish the standards that will help distinguish and differentiate the ecosystem enterprises established and operating in the Galapagos islands.

#### Output 3.1.2 The Galapagos community is mobilized towards a transformative climate action.
- A comprehensive educational approach to climate change will be integrated within the educational system of Galapagos (through Education for Sustainable Development), that includes innovative and pertinent education models, approaches, methodologies, and tools.

### Activity 3.1.2 Strengthen knowledge and foster engagement of the general public and key stakeholders on climate change adaptation and mitigation.

#### This activity aims to facilitate information, practical knowledge, tools, and outreach opportunities, to encourage the local and international communities' interest, support and active involvement in addressing climate change.
##### Contribute to the development of a comprehensive knowledge management system for the Programme in the short and medium term and will play an important basis for promoting deep engagement with stakeholders on climate change adaptation and mitigation measures. The activity will be implemented through a multi-stakeholder approach (See Appendix 3.6 on Behavioral Change), this activity aims to develop a comprehensive knowledge management system, including a database of information, tools, resources, lessons learned, and best practices.

#### The sub-activities are described in Annex 2 section 12.6.1, and include:
##### Develop a knowledge management and outreach digital platform that makes updated and relevant information, knowledge, lessons, and resources regarding climate change adaptation and mitigation measures available to the general public (targeting local, national and international audiences);
##### Develop and implement a communication strategy based on the multi-stakeholder approach for promoting deep engagement with stakeholders on climate change adaptation and mitigation measures;
##### Develop a behavior change campaign aimed at consumers of the food supply chain in Galapagos, including fishers, farmers, and other producers, to promote sustainable interventions. With the aim of fostering global connections and contribute to the search of scalability and replicability of successful interventions, this activity will also include the development of a communication strategy for the tourism sector (including tour operators, cruise lines, and hotels), as well as other sectors (e.g., agriculture, aquaculture, and fisheries) and other islands systems, other territories dependent on nature-based activities), in the form of workshops, seminars, and training sessions.

##### Facilitate the development of partnerships between non-formal education institutions and local communities, to connect the local community with the natural environment and climate change mitigation and adaptation measures. 54. The Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) is a specialized agency of the United Nations that leads international efforts to defeat hunger. With over 194 member states, FAO works in over 130 countries worldwide.

58. WWF is among the world's leading conservation organizations, with more than a half-century of experience and a global network that includes over 100 offices in 60 countries. WWF has worked on more than 300 projects around the world and more than 13,000 projects around the world. WWF's experience with the GCP is based in its role as an Accredited Entity.

### Conolophus SPV
62. A Conolophus SPV will be established for the purposes of implementing the Conolophus Project sponsored by Gran Solar and TollefSørensen, the concessionaires that won the public tender for the Conolophus Project.
63. GranSolar is a Leading Ecuadorian company founded in 2012 by a visionary group of investors who believe in the need to transform Galapagos into a microgrid that will make the islands the most positively impacted by renewable energy. The project will be implemented through the installation of a 5 MW solar farm, an Energy Central & Potentials: 14.5 MWh of photovoltaics + 39.9 MWh in batteries and their associated systems. In this project, GranSolar is supported by TollefSørensen, who has extensive experience in the energy sector and that motivates that in 2021 the electric generation from renewable energy sources should be increased to 90%.
64. The Conolophus Project will be implemented through a public-private partnership (PPP) with the objective of increasing the share of renewable energy in the Galapagos Islands. This project is part of the strategy of GranSolar to recover in the renewable energy sector, as an Independent Power Producer (IPP), Total Eren develops, finances, and operates projects that are aligned with the company's strategic goals. The Conolophus Project will be multidisciplinary and display a perfect grasp of all parts at stages of development of a renewable energy project: it is solar, wind, or hydroelectric. It is also an example of how to integrate different technologies in one project.
65. As of 2019, the Galapagos Islands have more than 3,500 MWp of renewable energy assets in operation or under construction and has over 4,000 MWp of potential for wind power generation. The Conolophus Project will be implemented by Total Eren Non-Evergreen Holding Company (NHC) or "Non-Evergreen", thereby diversifying its project portfolio and strengthening the company's position in the Galapagos Islands in particular.
66. Governance of the Programme
67. The Programme will have two levels of coordination:

Strategic coordination level
Programme Steering Committee
68. Maximum decision-making authority, consisting of high representatives of CAF, the Ecuadorian Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MIESD), the Government of Galapagos (EG) and the Governing Council of the Special Regime for Galapagos (COREG). - Verifying compliance of Programme implementation.
- Reviewing and approving the consolidated Annual Operating Plan.
- Reviewing and approving the Consolidated Annual Financial Plan.
- Reviewing and approving the Annual Performance Report (APR) according to the GCF's format.
- Review and approve the Semi-Annual Performance Report (SAP).
- Review and approve Major Changes according to the GCF’s Policy on Restructuring and Cancellation
- In case of any major changes, the Programme Management Unit will ensure that the Programme provides comments and recommendations.
- Be informed on the progress of the APR to be submitted to the Green Climate Fund.
- Review and approve the TORs of the Programme Management Unit (PMU).
69. Invite WWF, CFIN, CONAFOR, FAO as observers in the selection process of the Programme Management Unit. Hold meetings with these organizations at least once a year. The Steering Committee may be extraordinarily convened by the chair or at the request of any of the members.
70. The Chair of the Steering Committee will alternate annually between the MAATE and CAF through their designated representatives.

Operational structure of the Programme

92 The operational structure of the Programme will be based on the coordination of the Accredited Entity and the Executing Entities.


CAF’s functions as Accredited Entity

93 CAF will hold the role of the Accredited Entity of the Programme, based on its experience of successfully carrying out similar projects in the Caribbean and Latin American region. The existence of a central unit within CAF's sphere, will allow to guarantee compliance with technical standards and a close monitoring and follow-up by CAF to ensure consistent implementation of the Programme against the approved Project Document (PD) and the Strategic Plan against this plan. This takes corrective actions if necessary, during the Programme’s execution, ensuring it is carried out in accordance with the PD.

94 The external auditing and the incorporation of observations and lessons learned are guaranteed. All execution will be subject to an annual audit by internationally certified auditors, which will respect all understandings reached.

95 CAF will hold the role of the Accredited Entity for Programme supervision and have direct responsibility for fulfilling the duties and obligations of a GCF Accredited Entity. Anticipated legal arrangements:

The anticipated legal arrangements between the GCF, CAF, Existing Entities (CFN, CONAFIPs, FAO, WWF, Conservation International), and beneficiaries include:

CAF and GCF will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) for the Programme in the framework of the GCF. The FAA will be subject to the terms and conditions set out in the GCF’s General Terms and Conditions (the “Mandate”) of the proposed CAF-GCF Programme.

The GCF Secretariat, as the implementing entity, is responsible for providing governance, oversight, and quality assurance in accordance with its policies, procedures, and with the FAA and AMA.

The GCF Secretariat will be responsible for a pre-assessment process based on the criteria included in Annex 9—“Know your client”, and in most cases have pre-established financial arrangements with existing entities. The GCF Secretariat will also be responsible for entering into Long-Term Programme, where each due diligence will be presented to the CAF investment committee for review.

The GCF Secretariat will enter into a Financial Arrangement Agreement (FAA) or a Long-Term Agreement and Loan Agreements between the borrower and the lenders. Other contractual arrangements may be entered into as necessary including but not limited to: an equity sponsor/support/buyout contribution agreement, onshore/offshore security agreements and other ancillary agreements.

## B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

179. The Programme embraces the most fundamental transformation for the Galápagos Islands system in decades.

180. There is no other initiative with the vision, power, and determination to transform the Galápagos system and accelerate its transition towards a low emission and resilient economy within the frame of a post-pandemic green recovery. The Programme will be implemented by the Ecuadorian State, but it will have a considerable impact on the local economy, putting its major economic engine on hold while strengthening the case for an alternative future.

180. The long-term conservation of the Galápagos archipelago is a high priority for Ecuador; as it is the country’s most important natural asset and a key element in the global fight against climate change (UNEP, 2014). The Galápagos features exceptional conditions, setting it up to become a centre of climate knowledge and conservation. However, the current economic model is not sustainable for the long term. It relies on fossil fuels, all related institutions, and significant technological leap that currently cannot be financed by the Ecuadorian state or donors. In addition, the Galápagos economy is highly vulnerable to external shocks. The Programme will catalyse any change, coordinated among the different entities involved, to the extent necessary to adequately address these challenges.

181. In 2007, the government administration that took office began an aggressive construction process of building hydroelectric plants and petrochemical plants. As a result, Ecuador’s conventional energy demand is completely supplied by fossil fuels. The country has been unable to reduce its dependence on fossil fuels because the government’s budget for renewable energy plant constructions has reached its limits from 2008 (Galápagos Sustainable Energy Project, GSEP). Emissions have risen to their highest level in history.

182. With a deterioration of the fiscal deficit, a fiscal deficit of 3.5% of the GDP; a sovereign risk of 1,269 basis points and a banking index of 40.4%, it became progressively harder and more expensive for Ecuador to access adequate and recoverable financing from international financial institutions (IFIs) such as the World Bank and the Inter-American Development Bank. In addition, the country has been unable to reduce its dependence on fossil fuels because the government’s budget for renewable energy plant constructions has reached its limits from 2008 (Galápagos Sustainable Energy Project, GSEP). 187. In contrast to the current, the current Ecuadorian government fiscal budget is undermined, and the private Ecuadorian banks do not provide necessary loans for these kinds of ventures. Moreover, in Galapagos the average loan per capita provided by the private banking system is $243, while the national average is $1,304. In contrast, the GDP per capita in Galapagos is $5,879 compared to a national average of $16,000. This implies that the credit demand exceeds the credit supply. Since the lack of funding of supply is substantial, the GCF will not distort the market money costs. In this sense, the GCF can be considered as an additional source of financing for Galapagos.
189. On the contrary, the GCF can be considered as a complementary source of financing to the private sector. It can be concluded that requesting funds to the GCF in the form of concessional loans and grants is justified.

### B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Programme's exit strategy is based on or main 6 elements: 1) advocacy in the political administrative spheres, influencing the decision-making process; 2) the search for long-term impact of a subject of impact of funds for climate change-oriented investments; 3) the search for behavioral change and the commitment of beneficiaries; 4) the establishment of institutional capacity building; 5) the creation of a network of actors with a high potential of Galapagos as a demonstration case of transition towards a resilient and low-carbon development model.

1. Advocacy, preinstitutional articulation, and management plans
2. The Programme will seek to influence the activities currently carried out by the existing structures on the islands. The implementation of the project will modify the Annual Operational Plans of the various institutions involved in the programme (e.g., INAP, MAG, PNNG, etc.). In addition, the Programme will also seek to influence the work of other actors such as CIGREG, MAG, PNNG, among others.
3. Five years of intensive work with a climate change focus, for the implementation of the project, will be followed by a period in which the results and lessons learned from this experience will lead installed capacities in officials and regulations oriented towards the pursuit of adaptation and mitigation objectives. The Programme will seek to influence the institutional structure of the islands so that the results and lessons of the programme will be transferred and taken over by local institutions at the end of the programme.
4. It is expected that the transfer of knowledge, experience, and lessons learned from the project on adaptation and mitigation issues and their use first-hand the benefits and good results, will choose to promote this approach in a long-term perspective.

5. Throughout the programme, priority will be given to hiring local consultants. This strengthening will serve to facilitate that, in the future, they can be incorporated into local institutions and other projects and programmes that are developed on the islands.
6. The Programme will seek to influence the work of various actors (administration, parishes and municipalities: INAP, MAG, PNNG, universities, etc.) by transferring knowledge, experience, and lessons learned from this project will be transformative by inviting all actors to join a process with this perspective as its main focus.

7. The Programme will provide technical assistance in the form of various tools for administration, parishes and municipalities (INAP, MAG, PNNG, universities, etc.) and other institutions involved in implementing the actions, and generating new information (e.g., hydroclimatic monitoring) that can lay the foundations for long-term agreements.

195. In the same sense, the Programme will leave several planning instruments in place, which will constitute tools for long-term planning of adaptation to climate change on the islands. The Climate Change Action Plan (Activity 2.1.3.1) or a Climate Action Plan for the islands (Activity 3.1.3.1) will be left as long-term agreements.

and environment finance issues who are familiar with the reality of the Galapagos. As mentioned above, Galapagos has significant funds earmarked for conservation activities and with quite sophisticated mechanisms, but much less so in terms of climate change adaptation. The proposal will seek to engage with the Government to mobilize existing or new resources for climate action. Meetings, workshops, and working sessions will be organized to discuss the potential use of these funds. In addition to the analysis of the existing concession opportunities for the carbon economy, the blue economy will be analyzed, options will be designed and the role of the international hub that manages the financing mechanisms will be constantly fed to influence the allocation of resources.

203. Some issues that could be worked on would be, for example, the analysis of the potential use of the funds saved by the reduction in the number of visitors (driven by the investments of Component 1); on the other, the analysis of existing or emerging funds, such as the Galapagos Trust Fund, which is currently being discussed with the Government and the World Bank. The objective of the FRMS is to provide financial sustainability to the monitoring, control, and surveillance system of the Galapagos National Park. The FRMS will be a tool that will allow the government to manage the resources but nevertheless support actions in line with some of the activities in this proposal, such as the fight against invasive plants, the conservation of marine species, and the protection of the environment by reducing the number of visitors by exacerbating their dispersal.

204. At another important point, some of the Programme's activities are sustainable on their own, as is the case of the value chain activities, but others are more challenging to sustain, such as climate change monitoring or the conservation and management of marine species. The proposal will seek to identify opportunities for full funding through the aforementioned review of tariffs, fees, etc., state budgets, and new or existing funds.

- **The energy matrix change based on centralized and distributed power generation will make the electricity from each island's grid cleaner. - **Agriculture:** It is estimated that around 624 farmers will benefit directly from the project (119 female and 505 male). This represents approximately 37% of all households. Thus, the number of total beneficiaries is estimated to be around 1,872 persons.

Investments in ecosystem restoration, institutional strengthening, education, and dissemination activities will directly benefit almost 350 people. Technical assistance and capacity building activities will benefit almost 1,000 people, between tourism operators, local government officials, and other stakeholders. The project will also benefit the communities of key local agencies, ship owners, students; at least 230 local people will be trained to install and maintain the technologies promoted by the project, and the citizenship will benefit from awareness-raising actions for climate action.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Potential for scaling up and replication

The proposed Programme is highly scalable and replicable especially at the scale of islands and oceanic archipelagos where the economy depends on external resources (fish, food, and wood) whose economies are dependent on the nature-based tourism sector.

This type of project has a significant impact on the local economy in the Galápagos and makes it difficult to find, in the short term, other economic activity that takes over the role of tourism as driver of the economy. As the Programme is implemented, the number of jobs created will increase, especially in the tourism sector where demand grows; it buys inputs from other economic sectors, such as the artisanal fisheries and agricultural sectors, providing a multiplier effect on the local economy. The development of renewable energy technologies for the tourism sector will have many social benefits to the local economies and a considerable contribution to national and international conservation efforts.

The technical concept of the centralized PV project tackles specific aspects such as the intermittent nature of the renewable energy sources, the stability and reliability of electric grid operation, and the optimization and management of the power supply. The Programme also includes the development of a model for the construction of power plants based on renewable energy technologies in the small and isolated Santa Cruz – islands where access to electricity is limited or non-existent. This project will be implemented in the island of Santa Cruz – Baltra and provides the technical means for future generation projects integration.

The learning potential of this project lies in the demonstration of the benefits of renewable energy sources such as hotels, restaurants, and the demonstration of their benefits in terms of net generation and lower costs, accompanied by the development of a model for the construction of power plants based on renewable energy technologies. By channeling loans for these investments, will have a cascade effect on the population, and eventually a powerful demand for renewable energy sources.

Galápagos holds a leading position in terms of innovation as regards its regulatory and institutional framework, visitors' management, participatory management of resources, control and surveillance systems, and massive investment in research and development. The Programme is also aligned with the Government’s strategy to strengthen Ecuador's National System of Protected Areas, actively engaged in the exchange of knowledge and experiences with other countries that have similar ecosystems and environmental conservation initiatives. These conditions suggest that a successful program in Galápagos is likely to inspire a long-term paradigm shift towards renewable energy sources.

#### Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

A substantial portion of Lessons Learned will be based on the Programme's monitoring and evaluation activities, as well as knowledge management which will systematize the institutional reporting.

The Programme will implement a communication strategy (Action 3.1.2) centered around developing the necessary knowledge, values, attitudes, and capacities to make informed decisions about renewable energy sources. The Programme will also develop an awareness campaign to support a just society for the current and future generations. Climate change communication and social and behavioral change strategies will be developed in collaboration with local stakeholders. These are effective communication strategies and tactics are defined to influence people's awareness, understanding, concern and action on climate change.

Through Activity 3.1.2.3., the Programme will implement a communication and dissemination strategy to ensure a wide dissemination of the Programme in Galápagos and among the key stakeholders as well as the Programme beneficiaries. The specific activities include the development and distribution of promotional material of the Programme, such as websites, brochures, posters, etc. These materials will be used for promoting the Programme, communicating its results, and the systematization and dissemination of experiences and lessons learned from the Programme

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.83 | PAGE 102 OF 152

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

- **The market for climate change solutions remains underdeveloped in Galapagos**. This Programme will foster the development and implementation of projects that combine renewable energy generation and sustainable land use projects.
- To overcome barriers and create a long-term enabling environment for this kind of investments, the Programme will put in place a financial mechanism to provide grants to projects that are aligned with the national priority line and the technical assistance that will be provided to banks and beneficiaries. This will involve the development of processes and tools to identify, select, finance and monitor these types of projects, which will have a long-term and significant impact.
- Capacity building activities will guarantee that the market develops in a sustainable manner.

### Tourism operators, and the agricultural and fisheries sectors will benefit from capacity building, awareness raising and knowledge sharing

- These sectors will be supported to reduce their costs, increase their productivity and, at the same time, enhance the resilience of the ecosystems from which they depend.
- The broad-based participation of the private and public sector will be actively promoted by a Stakeholder engagement during the implementation stage. During the implementation of the Programme, a multi-stakeholder platform will be established to ensure that all relevant actors are involved in the decision-making methodologies such as the co-creation of best practices and the promotion of discussion spaces in which the private and public sectors can share their experiences.
- The Programme will also support the development of tools for the identification of projects, their involvement, and appropriation of the stakeholders in the future of nature-based tourism in the Galapagos.

### The Programme will directly contribute with the priority sets out by the Plan for Generation Expansion in the Galapagos Isolated System (PEGISAL), within which the Conchupio project is the largest project of its kind planned to be implemented on the islands. This project has been included as a priority under the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) and the National Energy Strategy (NES) (2013/18). The objective of the Program “Renewables Reformento - RENOVIA Second Phase” will be channeled through this funding proposal. This project is aligned with the second priority set out by the Plan for Generation Expansion in the Galapagos Isolated System, which focuses on the development of renewable energy projects in acclimatization and refrigeration areas in the tourist commercial sectors of the Province of Galapagos.

### Components of the Programme

- The programme will support the implementation of a series of actions to promote sustainable agriculture that fits the natural characteristics of the islands, also aligned with the second outcome area for Galapagos: “The Sustainable Development of Agriculture and Forestry in Galapagos”.
- The Programme will also contribute towards the development of the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) for Galapagos and the Fisheries Calendar defined by the Galapagos National Park Directorate (GNPD). The municipality of Puerto Baquerizo Moreno is a key actor in this strategy, as it is responsible for all land areas (including agricultural lands, as well as some of the high-ecological-value areas). Also relevant to this component are the actions that will be implemented within the framework of the zoning system of Galapagos protected areas), focuses on the conservation of the islands’ ecosystems.

### The Programme complements other regional governmental efforts

- led by the Governing Council of the Galapagos Islands (CGIG) and the National Government of Ecuador, in order to achieve the goals set out for this funding proposal. leading into an adequate integration of the funding within the regional long-term policy framework.
- This programme is aligned with the Plan for Generation Expansion in the Galapagos Isolated System, which provides response to a major gap Galapagos had in terms of emission reduction targets, concrete adaptation objectives and the need to develop renewable energy projects. The Governing Council of the Galapagos Islands affirmed that the integration of this programme with this Plan has already taken place and that the proposed

## D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### Environmental co-benefits

#### Protection of agricultural diversity, soils and water resources: measures proposed for the agricultural sector
- Measures to be implemented in local climate co-generation and sharing: greater sustainability and protection of the crops.
- Protection of water sources: control of the expansion of invasive species; increase of natural pollinators; reduction of soil erosion; improvement of soil quality; reforestation and afforestation on landscapes scale; pest reduction; conservation of unique species for the world. Improvement and diversification of agricultural production systems, including agroforestry.
- Soil protection: measures to be implemented in both protected and agricultural areas. The increase of massive species such as trees, shrubs, grasses, herbs, and flowers. Measures to be implemented under the 10th and 18th under program implementation scenario Also, the reduction or non-increase in food imports from mainland Ecuador.
- Protection of water sources: The inclusion of 300 ha of new resilient climate systems and 6,000 ha of agroforestation systems aims to improve the integrated management of water resources in Galapagos.

#### Reduction of the use of pesticides:
The Integrated Crop Management practices promoted will reduce the use of pesticides by 50%.


---
**Page 69**
---

# Pollution prevention in the agriculture sector

By constructing sustainable agroprocessing centers, wastewater and emissions that would normally be released at these stages will be treated or captured on site. Simultaneously, these treated waters will serve as biofilters for plantations. By increasing the demand for organic products, as raw materials for agroprocessing plants, farmers will have an additional income source.

# Restoration and protection of marine and terrestrial ecosystems

This Programme will work on the restoration and protection of coastal areas in San Cristóbal Island, Santa Cruz and Isabela Islands. These islands are considered exceptional biodiversity regions in the world. It will restore coral reefs, measures for adequate sea turtle nesting and large-scale reforestation.

# Monitoring of the impacts of human activities on the ecosystems

Information of environmental impacts such as pollution levels at the ecosystem scale of visitor sites and control sites regarding heavy metals, organic compounds, nutrients, etc., will be collected through sampling. The effects of these pollutants on fish populations and corals and mopepistacids, or the impact of diving practices, will be enhanced through the monitoring activities on marine ecosystems.

# Water management

These practices will be implemented in farms of San Cristóbal, Santa Cruz and Isabela islands at a scale that allows for their replication. The objective is to improve water use efficiency by adopting improved water management practices, such as the establishment of rainwater collection, water harvesting, treatment, storage and efficient irrigation system at a farm scale.

# Economic co-benefits

## New business models

The adoption of renewable energy technologies will provide opportunities for local value addition in the form of new businesses. The Programme will also open new business models linked to the emergence of citizens as renewable energy producers and energy providers. Also, these new schemes can contribute to creating jobs in the tourism sector.

## Additional income from energy savings

Energy efficiency and renewable energy projects will reduce the demand for fossil fuels and increase the energy bills and improve productivity at Galapagos which has the highest energy prices of the country.

## Additional income from crop diversification

The Programme will foster the diversification of the economic base of the communities by 5 to 10% through the introduction of new crops, livestock breeds and harvest waste such as biogas and biofertilizer. Silvopastoral systems that bring synergies between cattle and trees will be implemented in areas where there is a high potential for silvopastoral production with improved wildlife and productivity.

## Strengthening stronger value chains

The proposed activities will promote stronger and more dynamic value chains (substitution of production of meat, milk, coffee, vegetables, seafood, fish) that will allow actors to increase their income. The Programme will also contribute to the diversification of the economy by 5% which will be an improvement in terms of productivity. Crop productivity will increase within the first year of the project and until the end of its implementation. This will be achieved through the introduction of new crops, livestock breeds of seed production, with the implementation of the Program, an increase of 498.0 hectares is expected in 5 years. In addition to this, the Programme will contribute to the diversification of the economy by 10% through the Silvopastoral System. Using this system in the Silvopastoral System implemented in Galapagos, an average daily productivity increase of 24.8 kg per head is expected.

## Decreasing the uncertainty related to climate conditions

Farmers, authorities and functionaries will be informed about possible conditions that could harm their crops, animals, investments, and they will be able to make better decisions in time.

## Protecting the nature-based tourism resource by maintaining biodiversity

These areas may become an important source of income for local communities through ecotourism.

## Protecting incomes of fishers by protecting marine ecosystems

Scientific evidence suggests that the creation of marine protected areas can increase the productivity of yellow tuna and skipjack tuna around Galapagos through a spillover effect.

## Reduce losses due to climate impacts

Silvopastoral design provides multiple services to help farmers adapt to the new climatic conditions. The Programme will reduce mortality rates due to unbearable temperature and dehydration levels. High temperatures and low precipitation rates may also cause plagues to life, leaving cattle with no food.

## D

### Local banks with capacity to channel green investments in the identification, administration, monitoring and reporting of renewable energy (on-grid, off-grid, mini-grids) and other technologies for biofuel.

**Financing mobilized:** The Programme will leverage USD 26.85 million from private investments/private loans related to the centralized PV plant, and at least 10% of the co-financing that the beneficiaries of the credit line will invest.

### Social benefits

- **Number of Beneficiaries:** 33,000 corresponding to Galapagos population and 443,685 Ecotourism tourists and 91,251 Developing Country tourists in 5 years will be benefited by the Programme activities.
- **Job creation:** The Programme will create jobs for the installation of PV plants, their management, production, installation, and maintenance. Apart from the investments promoted through the loans, the Programme will provide additional income to the beneficiaries of the credit line. This will generate new employment opportunities in addition to job losses, because system complexity increases with higher energy efficiency. Also, agroprocessing stages will be employed for the processing of agricultural products (e.g., sugar cane, coffee, cocoa, bananas). These stages (pre-processing, roasting) will generate direct employment opportunities for the local population.
- **Social Economy:** The Programme will foster the implementation of companies within the framework of the Social Solidarity Economy. The beneficiaries of the credit line and the participants in the training workshops under the Social and Solidarity Economy framework will distribute the economic gains among coffee growers, according to the efforts they have made during their participation in the programme.
- **Healthy food environment:** The Programme will promote food systems that foster a diversified, balanced, and healthy diet for all inhabitants of the islands.
- **Water security:** By conserving and protecting the natural areas, the overall water security of the islands is enhanced. This includes not just the agricultural sector but also water supply for human consumption.

### Gender co-benefits

- **Women access to climate finance:** The Programme will provide local banks to have gender strategies, policies, procedures, and practices in place that ensure women’s participation in credit allocation, prioritization criteria in the credit line, and specific training will be provided to women to foster their participation.
- **Women participants in training and workshops:** The number of women and men attending programme components (training sessions, workshops) will be recorded. Data on the gender composition of participants will be collected. To ensure women’s participation in the workshops, specific budget will be allocated for the workshops budget. In addition to the training and workshop activities, a gender focal person will be appointed within each workshops; a survey will be addressed to know the best time day and when the workshops should be done.
- **The Programme will integrate gender considerations into all activities:** The Programme’s monitoring framework in addition, the Environmental and Social Specialist of the Project Management Unit (PMU) will ensure that gender is integrated into all aspects of the project. The PMU will monitor the implementation of the GAP, provide support with gender-related training and activities, and accompany the development of a Gender Action Plan within the Programme (for further information refer to the Gender Assessment and Action Plan in Annex 10).

### D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

- As described in Section B.1, the oceanic Galapagos archipelago is one of the most vulnerable places to climate change impacts on all aspects of life for its inhabitants and at all levels of its 33,000 residents, in addition to the 105,987 tourists entering the islands every year.
- Climate risks compound the threats posed by environmental change factors intrinsic to the development of the Galapagos archipelago. The impacts of climate change are already being felt on the islands: increased seasonality of rainfall, more intense ENSO events, sea level rise and acidification are manifested and will continue to be experienced in the future. These changes will have a direct impact on increasing unsustainable production and consumption patterns, overfishing and the spread of invasive species.

The document discusses the Green Climate Fund (GCF) funding proposal for the Galápagos Islands, focusing on how climate change impacts the agriculture, small-scale fisheries, and tourism sectors, which are major livelihoods in the Galápagos. It highlights that these sectors are highly dependent on terrestrial and marine ecosystems that will be heavily impacted by changes in sea surface temperature.

The document also mentions that major sources of income for the islands (e.g., fishing) are highly dependent on food and fish imports from mainland, making them vulnerable to climate change impacts such as changes in precipitation and temperature, disease outbreaks, and shifts in species distribution. The disruption of ecosystem processes and amphibian species can have significant economic consequences.

On the side of mitigation, the document notes that opportunities related to reducing greenhouse gas emissions (GHG) are related to sustainable land use and renewable energy generation and energy efficiency technologies. However, it points out that the Galápagos Islands lack the financial resources needed for these initiatives due to current low economic resources.

The document further explains that in the domestic market, conditions favoring these types of projects are not met because they require significant public investment. It highlights Ecuador's efforts to implement a technological leap through concessional financing and its impact on reducing GHG emissions by 2030. The document concludes with an invitation for further exploration into the financial feasibility of such projects in other sectors, including tourism, agriculture, fisheries, conservation, and energy.

### D.5 Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Galápagos Islands are governed by the Ecuadorian Constitution, recognizing natural rights and establishing special regimes for planning and development. It explicitly states that the government will promote the use of renewable resources in a sustainable manner while providing access to public services, preserving the environment, and maintaining food and water security.

The country's National Climate Change Strategy (2012-2025) establishes strategic and institutional bases for climate change mitigation and adaptation. The Galápagos Islands' National Adaptation Plan proposal is consistent with this strategy and related action lines concerning conservation and sustainable use of natural resources, as well as the development of a national system to respond to the impacts of climate change.

The document concludes by emphasizing that strengthening measures to promote energy efficiency and sovereignty will contribute to the gradual change in the Galápagos Islands' economy. It also highlights the importance of developing information on climate vulnerability at both territorial and sectoral levels, contributing to monitoring, reporting, and verification processes.


### The National Climate Change Mitigation Plan (PLANCCM) is currently in its initiation phase. This Plan is one of the climate change management instruments in accordance with the Organic Code of the Environment and its Regulations. It aims to provide a framework for national adaptation planning that is consistent with national capacities and circumstances, without harming the competitiveness and development of different sectors.

### Climate change mitigation actions are addressed through selected policies since 2009 via Executive Decree 1815, and through the Inter-institutional Committee on Climate Change established in 2010.

Executive Summary: 495. Furthermore, Ecuador has adopted the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development as a national policy through Executive Decree 371 from April 2016.

The Programme falls within the NDC presented by Ecuador in March 2018, in which the energy sector is vital in implementing climate change mitigation and adaptation actions. The present proposal contributes to the following NDC's lines of action and initiatives: boost the use of renewable energy sources; promote the development of a national policy for natural heritage including ACC, implementation of communication, dissemination and capacity-building programs that allow the awareness of actors in the agriculture and water sectors about impacts of climate change.

- Water sector: incorporation of climate change variables in technical feasibility and in the regulation and control of water resources, as well as in the development of adaptation strategies to ensure sustainable use of water for future; water in quality and quantity, and design and implementation of actions that contribute to increasing the resilience of ecosystems and communities.
- Natural Heritage: improvement of the public policy instrument for natural heritage including ACC, implementation of communication, dissemination and capacity-building programs.

The Programme is aligned with the Government Zero Emissions Strategy (GZES) aimed at gradually reducing the use of fossil fuels in vehicles, vessels, and thermoelectric energy generation, as well as to progressively replace them by renewable energy sources. The GZES also includes actions for climate change adaptation and mitigation in the tourism sector.

For the awareness and participation activities side, the Programme is aligned with the Paris Climate Change Agreement and the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development that "unambiguously recognize the importance of sustainable development and poverty eradication as essential elements to address climate change". The National Action Plan for Climate Empowerment (NACE) contained in article 6 of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) is also aligned with the Programme. The NACE includes a series of actions at national, regional, training, public awareness, public participation, public access to information, and international cooperation. They all play an important role in the implementation of the Programme.

At the National level, these activities are aligned with several local initiatives, the National Adaptation Plan Project (NAP), the National Climate Change Strategy (SNCC), the National Energy Efficiency Program (PENEE) and the Tiempo de Ecuador programme, promoted by the MAEE in collaboration with the Ministry of Education (Mineduc) and the Ministry of Tourism (MinTur).

Additionally, the Programme is aligned with the Environmental Organic Code, adopted in 2018, and includes climate change measures in local planning strategies.

The programme is structured around a multi-stakeholder engagement process led by local representatives representing stakeholders involved in different activities, allowing appropriation and alignment to contribute to relevant state development plans. ### Operating Expenses (OPEX): A benchmarking analysis with relevant reference projects has been carried out to further review operating costs (OPEX). This has been validated by CAF's internal technical team. However, please note that the figures presented in this financial model are indicative only and should be used as a guide for the figures presented in the financial model Annex 3: Financial Model Conceptualization.

The cost concepts included in the analysis are: operation and maintenance (O&M) costs; fuel costs; depreciation of equipment and transmission line, insurance, agent admin and facility costs (trusts); administrative and overheads, security and surveillance costs; and other miscellaneous costs.

### CAF financing:
- **CAF's main terms:** i) a variable interest rate of Libor (6m or equivalent SOFR term) + 5.8%, ii) a maximum loan amount of USD 20M, iii) a repayment period up to 14 years (with an amortization period up to 24 months), iv) a 7-year grace period (v) representing a door-to-door tenor of 21 years.

### GCF financing: the modeling is presented with a GCF fixed rate pricing of 2.50%** with similar tenors to those of CAF Financing.

### Leverage:
The model shows a leverage of 75% for the "Base Case" equivalent to USD 23.2 MM. However, CAF has the option to *promote* increase such match to reach an 80% leverage, depending on negotiations with Granville / Total debt and asset due ratings. Please note that are aware that assuming that GCF is prepared to disburse up to USD 15M, the leverage will be 67%.

### Scenarios:
- **Scenario 1** is the Base Case or Base Case - CAF @ 83% as per the Financial Model*, is the Base Case as described above.
- Scenario 2 is a sensitivity to the Base Case assuming an increase in CAPEX of 10% and finally Scenario 4 shows a decrease in power generation of 17% and consequent decrease in revenues.

## 245. The capital investment is USD 63MM

Excluding financing costs, the sponsors have calculated a total capital cost of USD 63 MM. The concessionaire is looking for a minimum of 70 % floor - 80 % maximum leverage. The funding structure will be based on the following assumptions: Project NPV = USD 152.4MM; Project IRR = 9.5%; and Project DSCR = 1.51%. The total project cost is USD 63 MM, which includes a debt component of USD 47.3 MM. These amounts are based on a 75% leverage. Any additional indebtedness over and above this leverage will be covered by the sponsors. The capital investment is also subject to a number of financial and economic variables that will be analyzed in detail under due diligence stage.

263. The G-Lab represents the methodological, operational and institutional framework required for the creation of an innovative financing mechanism that increases and coordinates governmental and non-governmental programs and projects for the promotion and development of sustainable fishing. The main objective of the G-Lab is to create a credit line for Galapagos small-scale fishers, cooperatives, artisanal fishermen associations, fishing companies and civil society entrepreneurs, in aspects related to social innovation for sustainable fisheries.

264. From the financial analysis it is obtained:

- A 10% interest rate of credit indicates that giving soft loans at US $35,000 to small fishing companies will result in a profitable investment that will produce appropriate for a typical fishing company, a return on capital of around 8%. However, this scenario is only possible if the cost of the capital is below 12%.
- A sensitivity analysis and different scenarios, the Incentive Program of the credit line is only possible if the cost of the capital is below 9.5%, which would be a return on capital of around 7%. Both conditions are non-market conditions, since the interest rate on corporate loans at which the small fishing companies can obtain financing in the market is currently above 12%.
- The lending rate is around 25%.

The G-Lab and Incentive program have identified at least three business models with the potential to promote climate-smart and sustainable small-scale fisheries and systemic changes in the Galapagos fishery.

a) Seafood export company: The Shippers Association proposes the creation of the "Galapagos Seafood Company" (GSC), which would be a joint venture between the GSC and the Shippers Association, with 50% of vessels owned by the members of the Association. The GSC aims to create an independent commercial competitor in the international market for Galapagos seafood products. The GSC will have access to the supply chain and the weak management capacity of current fishing cooperatives. The GSC offers market information on prices, demand, and quality standards to its members, which are currently unaware of the supply surplus. The GSC plays an intermediary role in the commercialization of tuna caught by the Shippers Association. The GSC will be able to offer a higher price for Galapagos tuna than the current market price to the fisherman for his product plus the logistics expenses to place the product in international markets. The GSC will have a 10-year term, with an initial investment of US $923,520; recovery is 24 months. Sensitivity analysis shows that if the GSC returns on investment could be varied between 8% and 16%, it would be profitable for the company to invest in this project.

b) Value-added fishing company: An example prototype from the past is "Blue Fish". This seafood company was established by a local entrepreneur, who had previously worked with the Galapagos National Park. Azul produced manufactured smoked food products using yellowfin tuna, which was sourced from local fishermen and processed at the company's facility in Puerto Ayora. The company used size standards related to catch size and gear to sell their catch to Blue Fish. Once processed, the value-added product was sold on the international market. The prototype of this business model was established by a company contributed to reducing the fishing pressure on overexploited coastal fisheries and advanced an alternative economic activity for fishermen in Galapagos. The new local entrepreneur is trying to restart this prototype again in Isabela and Puerto Ayora, with the support of the G-Lab. The new company has invested US $108,500; it will have a period of 10 years, if the NPV is positive reaching US$139,000. The investment could be recovered in less than one month, and the project would generate an annual profit of US$267,000 after deducting production costs. Therefore, training and capacity building grants should focus on helping Pesquero Azul gain access to markets.

c) Fisheries by-products company: This is the least explored prototyple, but the most promising to promote a commercial activity that would generate income for fishermen in Galapagos. For example, fish waste products could be used to produce fertilizer and other products. In Galapagos, it was estimated that 2.2 t of fish waste per day are produced at Puerto Ayora, Galapagos, consisting of the remains of fish discarded by fishermen after processing; the fish waste is commonly treated as garbage and disposed to the landfill for disposal.

### Agriculture

The program offers different activities that include the implementation of agricultural practices that allow the cultivation of species throughout the year that will adapt to climate change. Likewise, it seeks to develop forestry diversification and the creation of processing service centers are strengthened so that the population has access to healthy processed products.

273. In the different intervention practices proposed, the financial analysis included the establishment of their implementation costs, the estimated income generated by each activity, as well as the expected number of households or practices or production involved.

### Inputs

Regarding the estimation of the cost of the products, the following was adopted: to establish the projected utility of the community seed bank, the seed production potential per year and the average sale price were used. For the estimated income generated by each activity, the average sale price for each product that is obtained in each case were considered. For biofertilizers, the increase in biomass production was considered and associated with the cost of the organic fertilizer to be replaced. The cost of the inorganic fertilizer that will replace it in the implementation of the practice was used as the estimated price.

For the estimation of income generated by each activity, the average sale price for each product obtained for slaughter and milk processing activities such as cheese and yogurt. In the different cases, to estimate the sales volume, the number of households or practices involved were considered.

275. Regarding the use of labor: if the task to be performed has a certain capacity, the hiring of full-time technical personnel is considered; if the task to be performed is periodic, the staff is hired through salary.

276. In this case, the implementation of agricultural practices that allow the cultivation of species throughout and practices that intervene in several farms and at the same time it has the creation of plants for the use of many beneficiaries at once. The estimated income generated by each activity was considered as well as the number of households or practices involved.

The program execution period was set at 5 years. The NPV of the project's general agricultural practices would reach USD$1,132,053 while the feasible IRR would be 26.4%.

---

277. The financial analysis at the final beneficiary level was taken as an example of the activity "Implement strategies to improve livestock / meat and dairy value chain" on San Cristobal island and results in an IRR equal to 8.64% and a NPV at a discount rate of 10% of US$1,531,660.05 and the payment is recovered after 6 years. In a sensitization scenario GCF if you find credit supply in the project start date as the high rate, the investment is not profitable.

278. As a co-benefit of agricultural interventions, approximately 251,960 tons of CO2eq from agriculture areas will be avoided.

279. All this will result in a nutritional benefit for the population of Galapagos. Incorporating these practices will support both food security and nutrition as well as improving livelihoods and developing resilient agricultural systems that currently affect vulnerable Galapagos farmers.

280. Likewise, practices to ensure the availability of water throughout the year support the supply of crops in times when there is no rain. This will also contribute to reducing the vulnerability of Galapagos farmers.

281. GCF support in the form of grants and loans are key elements in reducing market barriers, improving livelihoods and developing resilient agricultural systems that currently affect vulnerable Galapagos farmers.

282. The project has a five-year implementation period. It is expected that all conditions will be established for the project to have continuity beyond the five years of interventions.

283. Without the project, neither the benefits nor the costs would occur. And without the support of the GCF, the project that has workshops and monitoring is not viable and these are necessary for the success of the program.

## Scale

Although although a general level adaptation exists in the Galapagos Islands to climate change impacts, it is important to note that there are significant differences between the Eastern and Western regions of the Galapagos. The Eastern region includes the Galapagos Conservation Trust (GCT) for the acronym in Spanish (Fundación para la Conservación de las Islas Galápagos), a regional initiative (Ecuador, Costa Rica, Panama). The GCT is committed to conservation and sustainable use, considering as core areas the marine protected areas of the Galapagos Islands, Coba, Galapagos and Cocos formed as Network of Marine Protected Areas.

### Output 3.1.2

The Galapagos community is mobilized in a participatory manner through dialogue, training, workshops and dialogues with indigenous communities and local authorities. Communication on climate change adaptation and mitigation is strengthened.

The OAAM works through the establishment of joint governmental regional strategies supported by civil society organizations and non-governmental organizations.

Currently, UN OAR does not consider climate change related issues.

## Replicability

Today, Galapagos' main livelihoods (agriculture, small-scale fishing, and tourism) are vulnerable to climate change. There is a lack of accessible, timely, understandable information about climate change, leading to poor climate-related decision-making and evidence-based decision making. Decision-makers need to be agents important of action when confronted with climate change and access to financial incentives.

Adaptive capacity of farmers through achieving participatory and integrative farm planning processes can be replicated. The strategic options for climate change adaptation measures can be replicated directly by the programme, resulting in enhanced resilience of the Galapagos food system. Fishers, farmers and tourism operators need to be reached directly by the programme, resulting in increased awareness of the benefits, combined with climate change adaptation strategies and farm incentives.

Output 2.1.5. Enhanced institutional capacity for climate-resilient planning and development.

Output 2.1.3. Adaptive co-management of the Galapagos food system through participatory management frameworks and systems. Key results include: improved understanding of participatory methodologies and process for marine spatial

Output 2.1.4. Increased adoption of climate-smart small-scale farming practices, with clear evidence of the impacts and benefits of good fisheries management practices collected.

#### Institutional and regulatory framework
The country has climate change policies with greater capacity to address climate change in land use and marine sectors.

#### Core indicator 7: Degree to which the GCF portfolio contributes to market diversification of low carbon solutions at the sectoral, local or national level
The market for climate change solutions is underdeveloped in Galapagos. The Programme will foster the development of a new market for low carbon energy and sustainable land use projects.

However there are no climate change policies in Galapagos.

The Programme will catalyse a shift from business as usual towards climate resilient and low carbon ecotourism in Galapagos. Economic activities widely recognized as the most sustainable are those that generate more income than either system of extractive production, processing, and consumption and for which there is a long term and sustained market demand. Capacity building activities will guide us to ensure these market impacts are sustainable.

The Programme will put in place concessional technical assistance that will be provided by the Fund and will involve the development of processes and procedures to assist the Galapagos people's transition to new jobs. On another hand, the programme tends to generate additional jobs for local populations as complexity increases with higher value added activities. Also, approaching these activities from a gender perspective can provide direct employment opportunities for the local population.

### Zero (Z) Behavioral Change Strategy aimed at consumers of the food system in Galapagos.

The sustained participation of the private and public sectors in the implementation stage is promoted by a Stakeholder engagement plan during the implementation stage.
During the finalization phase of the Programme, a multi-stakeholder approach will be pursued to create common ownership for programme ownership.

Investments promoted in the 5 years of implementation of the Fund act in the areas of formal and informal education and communication to promote awareness towards collective climate action. This is an important element in Galapagos where the regulatory and institutional frameworks for climate-responsive planning are weak, impacting the sustainability of the program (see Outcome 3:1).

Environmental methodologies such as the co-creation of best practices and the promotion of discussion spaces between the private sector would be included, will bring us closer to the shared responsibility, active participation, and appropriation of the stakeholders in the future of nature-based tourism in the Galapagos.
