E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Document of the sustainability strategy developed

Document describing the design and implementation of the financing mechanisms.

Reporting from Executing Entities (EEs) to CAF

CAF will set up a monitoring framework at the Programme level. For more details, refer to Annex I: Monitoring and Evaluation Plan.

284. The Programme will apply the standard procedures established by CAF for monitoring and evaluation of investment operations. Based on the proposed results and a risk assessment, CAF will determine the frequency of monitoring visits required during the implementation phase as well as the timing of these visits for programme execution. In coordination with CAF, EEs will complete and maintain all information, indicators, and parameters necessary for the preparation of Programme reports, including annual report, midterm review, and final evaluation.

285. It will be the responsibility of CAF or CCAF in close collaboration with the technical assistance of FAO and WFP to ensure that the sub-borrower is eligible for funding from the Programme in accordance with the programme’s eligibility criteria. It will be the responsibility of FAO and WFP to ensure that the beneficiaries are eligible for funding under the terms of the loan agreement, in accordance with the programme’s eligibility criteria. Monitoring of disbursements for eligible expenditures will be reviewed by CAF in coordination with EEs; CAF may schedule monitoring visits to inspect and verify the proper use of resources and compliance with contractual conditions of the Programme with regards to the use of funds.

286. The processing procedures will include a review of the indicators achieved in each phase and evaluation of progress towards achievement of proposed targets. The indicators to be monitored will be those included on the log frame, as well as other indicators that are relevant for monitoring the achievement of results.

287. EEs will collect the necessary data for monitoring and present annual reports to CAF. Beneficiaries of the loans will also be trained to be able to contribute with the information required by CAF in order to monitor progress on loan disbursements, as well as other relevant indicators. In some cases, CAF will make calculations required for some indicators based on data provided by the local banks in the annual reports. The EEs’ own information systems will also be used by CAF to analyze whether they are sufficient and appropriate for monitoring the proposed indicators.

288. CAF will deliver these annual reports within thirty (30) calendar days after the end of each year of the Programme’s implementation. The reports will include information on the status of the programme, as well as additional information regarding the use of the resources. CAF will be entitled to request additional information, if necessary. In addition to the annual reports and the scheduled activities for monitoring of the operations described above, CAF will contract an independent evaluation firm to conduct a final evaluation of the Programme at the end of its implementation period (i.e., after all funds have been disbursed – whichever occurs first). Finally, CAF will present a final report to CAF up to six months after the day of the last disbursement and CAF may contract an independent third evaluation. The EEs’ final reports shall contain all relevant information to assess objectives of the Programme and target for each year.

CAF will provide annual progress reports on the status of the funded activity throughout the relevant reporting period, based on the above-mentioned monitoring framework.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Risk Factor 1: Tourism industry measures (see 3 pages)

292. The main Programme risks are related to: Placement risk through the loan line caused by the non-reactivation of the tourism sector in the islands; Climate technologies not performing as expected; Resistance to change; Local communities and stakeholders not being adequately involved; and Inadequate project management. These risks are low to moderate in nature and CAF expects they can be largely mitigated through the implementation of planned Programme measures.

293. CAF, as Accredited Entity, will ensure that Executing Entities, local banks, and beneficiaries comply with the Environmental and Social Performance (ESP) is all projects, which include social and local food modules.

## Selected Risk Factor 1: Placement risk from the loan line caused by the non-reactivation of the tourism sector in the islands

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Critical | Low | Medium |

### Description
294. The pandemic has severely affected the islands' economy and there are many doubts as to how the tourism sector will reactivate, especially given average recovery rates of 50% in 2021-2023, while it is expected that there will be a good reactivation - projections may change, and the scenario may be worse than expected.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
295. The strategy at the Galápagos level is one of local reactivation. The fact that the islands are 1,000 km from mainland has many drawbacks as those described in this proposal (dependence on external inputs), etc., but it also has advantages such as the unique biodiversity and natural environment which makes them a very attractive destination for nature tourism, which is precisely the type of tourism that is expected to be most in demand due to the pandemic.

296. Also, based on the results of the survey conducted with part of the market study, there is confidence in the interest of tourists to visit the islands.

297. The mitigation measures for this placement risk correspond to the activities related to Technical Assistance, awareness raising and citizen science. These are all actions that will be carried out through the project methodology with awareness-raising, citizen science and communication activities, which will help raise stakeholder awareness about the importance of tourism in the islands.

298. Regarding how the CAF loan will make an impact on the progress of the Programme's activities, it is important to note that the Galápagos strategy is one of local reactivation; therefore, the loan will not have a direct impact on the first stages of the project implementation and, as such, ways to continue with the implementation of the project will be evaluated in the help of virtual tools where possible.

## Selected Risk Factor 2: Climate technologies do not perform as expected

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
299. Renewable energy, energy efficiency, land use change and ecosystems climate change projects implemented may not perform as expected; if this occurs, they will have a lower impact on climate change than estimated.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
300. The Programme is specifically designed to mitigate these risks through:
- Activity 13.1.1 - Technical Assistance Facility for energy efficiency
- Technology assessment and selection of technologies in Component 2.
- Programme Management

301. Furthermore, the project building will include development of technology-specific project assessment protocols. Also, in terms of Environmental and Social performance, CAF, as Accredited Entity, will ensure that

## Selected Risk Factor 3: Resistance to change

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

### Description

367. There could be a risk of resistance to change and a history of poor performance of previous projects in the more complex areas of the country, such as the Galapagos National Park. This is particularly true for the latter case, due to a generational issue (there is a majority of older people) and in the case of the latter, due to a tradition of being a rentier contribution group.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

368. It is necessary to seek an execution of the activities that takes care of the relations with these groups and works closely with them. This will be done through regular visits, providing technical assistance, following up visits, and communicating through visits internationally. The Programme’s Stakeholder Engagement Plan contemplates this.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: Local institutional capacities for Programme execution

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

### Description

365. There is a possibility that local institutions have difficulty in supporting a Programme of this size and diversity and that the needs for its execution exceed their capacity.

## Selected Risk Factor 5: Implementation

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

368. Successful implementation depends on the overall technical and operational capacity of the AE, the PMU, the Executing Entities, and their interactions with the local financial institutions and the Governmental partners that will be engaged in the Programme. The risk is rated as low because there are several instances through which mitigation can take place during implementation, although it is rated low, due to the demonstrated ownership of the project by local institutions and the long track record of implementation.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

369. The Programme will have several instances through which mitigate this risk, namely: the Steering Committee and its sub-committees will be responsible for monitoring progress, reporting, and information sharing; best possible solutions will be shared. The long track record of CAF and the Executing Entities brings high capacities to solve problems.

The President of the CUREG was the last to take office and the AE is organizing an agenda to present the programme in front of the new government.

310. Even though then the new government is now established, Ecuador's experience in Ecuador says that Ministers can change very often, and this always alters the implementation stability of a programme.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Framework for environmental and social risk management (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### 319 An Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) was developed for the Programme. Please refer to Annex E - E&S document corresponding to the E&S category.

#### 320 The ESMF is based on the IFC Performance Standards, CAFI, FAO, and WUFG safeguard standards. An alignment exercise was carried out to identify the scope of the analysis and the particularities of each standard in relation to the Programme’s activities. This resulted in a set of environmental and social risks and impacts with high adverse environmental or social risks and/or that impacts are diverse, irreversible, or unprecedented, due to the fact that they are related to climate change, biodiversity loss, and ecosystem services.

#### 321 The Programme seeks to implement a multi-sectoral and cross-cutting climate initiative to catalyse a transformative shift towards a self-sustaining low-carbon island system with greater adaptive capacity to climate change impacts.

#### 322 The Programme envisages a holistic approach, combining climate change mitigation and ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) measures. These are aimed at reducing the vulnerability of small islands to GHG emissions in the islands, while at the same time a highly vulnerable destination.

### 323 What are the environmental and social risks that are evoked at all levels?

#### (a) Environmental: i) Loss or affection on the flora and fauna of the poor plant conservation areas ii) Environmental contamination due to the use of fertilizers and pesticides iii) Soil erosion iv) Use of non-native seeds for strengthening the food chain.

#### (b) Social: i) Displacement of local communities, especially women, youth, and elderly from program activities and benefits due to structural circumstances. ii) Exclusion of potential beneficiaries due to lack of land ownership or inability to access credit facilities iii) Lack of awareness on the importance of sustainable livelihoods iv) Lack of appreciation and commitment of local people for sustainable livelihoods.

#### (c) Economic: i) Environmental management plans for constructions that establish guidelines and procedures to manage the introduction of construction materials, waste disposal, and other environmental aspects. ii) Establishment of monitoring actions for the equitable distribution of benefits and access to loans, and knowledge generation. iii) Establishment of mechanisms for the enhancement of livelihoods through the development of local enterprises iv) The enhancement of resources within the framework of sustainable tourism v) Contribute in terms of improving the legal framework for the protection of natural resources.

#### 327 The stakeholder involvement plan included the definition of key stakeholders defined for the entire tourism chain. These are: i) Local communities ii) Government iii) Private sector iv) International organizations at each stage on the linkage and participatory generation of feasibility inputs to the project. In addition, there is a role for the government in terms of providing information and support during the preparation phase and the Public Information Consultation (PIC) process.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 329 Please find the full gender assessment and project level gender action plan as Annex E.

#### 330 The Programme faces the challenge of aligning the tourism chain, which ranges from understanding the dynamics of agriculture, livestock and fishing production, identifying gender relations in the services, as well as recognizing the role that women play in the development of the tourism sector.

#### 332 The methodology used consisted, at first, in identifying the legal regulatory framework and the progress made in its implementation. This was followed by a review of the existing policies and strategies related to gender equality and responsibility in agricultural production. In terms of fishing activities, the number of women involved in extractive activities is very low compared with men. Women are also responsible for waste collection and recycling as well as waste from tourism. In terms of both river and land transport activities, women play a minor role. Also, in terms of garbage collection, women do not exist. At the level of product commercialization and services, women participate in a higher percentage.


---
**Page 137**
---

334\. That said, the link with resource control is limited because the local population must move within the conservation "rules and regulations" of the Gagapungo National Park, which is why the possibilities may be limited. Women have fewer options for involvement in productive activities. These dynamics often affect women in terms of economic and social empowerment.

335\. In terms of the environmental dynamics of the islands, two patterns are evident that affect the quality of life of its inhabitants:

* **Access to food:** Access to healthy, affordable, and accessible food on the islands. Due to the high dependence on food from the sea, there is a risk of malnutrition in a short time horizon.
* **Energy access:** Energy access is also a major issue for the islanders.

336\. In the framework of renewable energy and gender, the relationship is complex because there is no direct "gender" component to renewable energy. However, women are more likely than men to be interested in and of interest in switching to clean energy. However, the need for all at standards to lower or reduce their energy consumption is a challenge.

337\. Once the gender analysis has been carried out, a general goal is established according to each of the components identified (see Annex 8).

Some of the main activities of the Gender Action Plan are:

* **Awareness raising:** Awareness raising and training (implementing organisations' organisations) (workshops for adoption of gender perspectives for the effective participation of women in the programme).
* **Sensitization:** Sensitization of all stakeholders.
* **Design of positive actions:** For the incorporation of women in the tourism sector, especially in areas that are not traditionally dominated by women.
* **In general, women-led enterprises will be particularly supported with technical assistance to access loans.** For instance, women's groups can be established and/or strengthened (Acting 1.2.3).
* **Strengthening women's knowledge of agrodiversity as subjects of change:** Focus on the role of women in agriculture and their contribution to food security.
* **Focus on women's economic empowerment through relevant income-generating activities for women and men who are not in their traditional role to be able to achieve more and better income.**
* **Women's participation in decision-making processes:** Women's participation is essential, especially when it comes to the appropriation and empowerment of rights and environmental governance of their organizations.
* **Women's representation in the value chain for women in the Gagapungo value chains.

Please refer to Annex 8 for details.


### F.3\. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

339\. As per the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), CAF, as the Accredited Entity, shall be responsible for the overall management of the GCF Facility. This includes the development and implementation of policies, procedures, guidelines, policies, and procedures, including administering and managing the use of GCF Proceeds, as well as the monitoring and reporting on the results.

340\. CAF will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement with the GCF for the Loan and the Grant, which will describe the scope of work to be carried out by CAF in relation to the Facility. The Funded Activity Agreement shall include the CAF's Manual for the Credit and Equity Investment Process (MINDCRIT-017) which has been reviewed by the GCF and approved by the GCF Board.

341\. As described in section B.4 Implementation arrangements, CAF will enter into loan agreements or amend existing loan agreements with the GCF to provide funding for the Facility. The Funded Activity Agreement shall include the CAF's Manual for the Credit and Equity Investment Process (MINDCRIT-017) which has been reviewed by the GCF and approved by the GCF Board.

342\. As described in section B.5 Technical Assistance, CAF will provide technical assistance to implement Component 1, and by FAD and WWF in the case of Components 2 and 3. For the Technical Assistance executed by CAF, the regulations that apply are those of the GCF Facility. In the case of Technical Assistance provided by FAD and WWF, the regulations that apply are those of the respective implementing organisations.

343\. In the case of CAF, procurement processes will follow the last version of CAF's Manual of Procurement of Goods, Construction Works, and Services (MINDCRIT-018) which has been reviewed by the GCF and approved by the GCF Board. The manual sets out the principles for the selection of suppliers and establishes the thresholds for the procurements.

344\. FAD and WWF will be undertaken on the basis of competition and is based on the fundamental principles of Best Value for Money, fairness, transparency, economy and effectiveness. Additional objectives of FAO procurement are to:

* **The GAF considers women-owned enterprises as per IFG definition:**
    * (A) At least 51% of the shares in a company are owned by one or more women.
    * (B) At least 20% of the shares in a company are owned by at least two women. (C) At least 30% of the board of directors is composed of women, where a board exists.

## G4. Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all documents submitted to it, unless the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

The funding proposal will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board. Subject to the IDP and any applicable confidentiality agreements, the accredited entity is responsible for ensuring that its disclosure of the funding proposal complies with the IDP. The Board may request additional information from the accredited entity if it has concerns about the adequacy of the disclosure.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, containing confidential information.

### Disclosure of funding proposal

- **No confidential information:** The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF. No information is being provided in confidence.
  
  - **With confidential information:** The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF; as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity discloses a copy of the funding proposal and an accompanying redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

  - **Full copy for internal use:** The GCF will maintain the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure agreement.
  
  - **Redacted copy for disclosure:** A redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, containing confidential information.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP185

## Proposal name: Climate Change: The New Evolutionary Challenge for the Galapagos

### Accredited entity: Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF) / Development Bank of Latin America

### Country/(ies): Ecuador

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. **The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:**

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| Programme expects to reduce approximately 600,000 tCO2eq in lifespan from energy; more than 50% of the total area (137 km²) of marine ecosystems made more climate resilient. | Category A environmental and social impacts are expected to be managed by the accredited entity |
| The programme will allow Galapagos Islands to reduce its overall energy dependence on diesel imports, while also promoting renewable energy use; it will contribute to capacity building and knowledge management on climate change in the Galapagos, while promoting climate-resilient development strategies. | Programme implementation period is only five years. With a longer implementation period, there would be more achievement opportunities for activities could evolve. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum VII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations", respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

3. The Galapagos Islands are considered a living laboratory of evolutionary processes. Its diversity and the uniqueness of its species, volcanic origin, and geological dynamics enable these processes to be studied in situ. The islands have been declared a UNESCO World Heritage Site that does not exist anywhere else in the world. This makes the Galapagos a unique and globally important site for the natural heritage of humanity (the area was declared a Natural Heritage Site by UNESCO in 1978). The Homologation Act of 2004 put an end to climate change.

4. Climate change impacts the Galapagos Islands' marine and terrestrial ecosystems, the inhabitants and their livelihoods as well as their economic activities such as agriculture.


---
**Page 147**
---

# Fisheries

Fisheries are threatened through increased temperatures, changed rainfall seasonality, extreme hydro-meteorological events expected to result in reaquatization, increased sea surface temperature and sea level rise.

## Galapagos Islands

- **33,000 people live on the Galapagos Islands**, and the estimated number of tourists visiting the islands has jumped (post-COVID-19). Tourism, mainly nature-based tourism, is very important, accounting for 86 per cent of the local economy.

### Environmental Impacts

The Galapagos marine and terrestrial ecosystems suffer from climate change impacts that are exacerbated by human activities. These include overfishing, unsustainable hunting, pastoral and fishing practices as well as forest tourism. Anthropogenic impacts include, for example, habitat destruction, pollution, introduction of invasive species to new sites and trails resulting in disturbance to plants and animals. Unsustainable land management and land abandonment also contribute to the degradation of terrestrial ecosystems. Overfishing contributes to ecosystems being stressed. In all sectors, the introduction and spread of invasive species are a major threat. The tourism sector is particularly vulnerable as it relies on fuel and food, which is unsustainable, and the logistics contribute to unnecessary stress on the marine and terrestrial ecosystems.

### Local Population

The local population manages the local practices in Galapagos in terms of the local population's ecosystem management and energy use, but also with regard to the many tourists who visit the islands. The Galapagos Conservation Trust (GCT) has identified three components: 1. Energy matrix change in the Galapagos archipelago; Component 2. Building climate resilience for livelihoods in the Galapagos; and Component 3. Sustainable mechanisms for climate resilience and low emission measures.

#### Component 1: The central power plant, Conolophus investments in decentralized power supply and its energy efficiency. It also includes environmental assistance.

- **Component 2:** This component involves training of local technical staff in aspects regarding climate change, including appropriate climate impact monitoring systems for the Galapagos archipelago. It also includes the rehabilitation of degraded terrestrial ecosystems through the rehabilitation of ecosystems to strengthen their climate resilience, as well as measures to improve the local food value chain.

- **Component 3:** This component involves building structures that maintain the climate resilience and low emission measures. This component will compile an ecosystem management plan for the Galapagos archipelago, including a monitoring system, strengthening knowledge and fostering engagement among the local citizens and tourists as well as mainstreaming climate change in regular training sessions and planning instruments.

### Program Budget

The total budget of the program is USD 10 million (USD 9.5 million from GCF and USD 500,000 from other sources). The project's internal rate of return (IRR) is 1-3, given the Conolophus project, which has Category A related risks. All other activities in the program have Category B related risks.

The total budget for the Conolophus project is USD 25 million (USD 20,541,166 and grant USD 4,739,134) as sought from GCF. Other financial contributors include senior loans (CAF - USD 32,800,000; World Bank - USD 1,000,000; Inter-American Development Bank - USD 560,000; bid winner Gramática/Fetal Eres, and USD 3,880,000 from various small local beneficiaries' credits).

### Types of Loans

Two types of loans are proposed: one commercial loan to the Conolophus project, where GCF provides USD 20 million out of the total investment of 63 million (as mentioned, the equity part of this loan is USD 1.5 million). The other loan is a grant from GCF for USD 4.7 million and is still not settled between the AE and GCF, but the aim is to complement the CAP contribution in a way that renders a calculated internal rate of return (IRR) below the present industry standard


---
**Page 148**
---

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1 - Energy metrics change in the Galapagos archipelago (total cost: USD 76.2M; GCF ID: GST2798M or 36.6%)

This component will increase low-emission energy access and reduce the energy consumption of households in the Galapagos Islands by facilitating access to finance and technical assistance for renewable energy generation (PV plant and micro-distributed PV generation systems) and energy efficiency measures (e.g., LED lighting, water heaters, and refrigerators). The tourism sector is particularly targeted since it is identified as a driving force for the islands' economy. Farmers are also eligible for the micro-distributed PV generation credits.

Under this component, the program will finance the following: A 14.8 MW centralized renewable energy power plant (PV) in Galapagos; a 20 kW PV system at the airport; and include a 649 MWh battery storage system, a switching station at 34.5 kV, 49 kilometer sub-transmission lines, and 17 kilometers of distribution lines. The project will also support micro-distributed renewable power generation systems (small-scale solar PV systems) in tourism/commercial, agricultural and fisheries sectors, managed by Corporación Financiera Nacional del Pacífico Sur (CFNAPS), and provided through local banks in the Galapagos; credit lines to support optimization and replacement of inefficient equipment in hotels and restaurants, managed by CFNAPS and provided through local banks in the Galapagos; and credit lines for energy efficiency project development, implementation and related issues.


---
**Page 149**
---

20. Estimated emission reductions amount to 584,169 tCO2e over the lifetime of the project, which renders a GCF cost per tonne of USD 113.7/ton CO2eq.

21. In 2020, the Government of Ecuador delegated development of the project to private national and/or equity companies for a period of 25 years, thus becoming the concessionaire. The agreement includes a financial management framework to assure transparency and compliance in the financial management, quality, and sustainability of energy generation projects. The GCF assessment concludes that the financial structure is designed for viability. However, the Ecuadorian financial market is unable to accrue the required capital at sufficiently beneficial terms, as CAF found upon issuing its letter of intent to the bidders.

22. The winning bid offered to sell electricity at a price of US$ 0.45-0.99 cents/kWh, which was agreed by the Government of Ecuador and the private sector. However, the GCF for the concessionaire would still need money. The bid winner is requested to sign an agreement with CAF to provide concessional financing (see Table 2 in Appendix 1, page 30). Our assessment concludes that GCF proceeds are motivated. First and foremost, without GCF there will not be sufficient commercial capital lending available in Ecuador. Secondly, with concessional funding, the project would have a debt service coverage ratio of approximately 4x, which would be much below the industry rate of between 17 per cent and 19 per cent according to the feasibility study. Thirdly, the interest rate on the concessionaire’s loan is heavily influenced by the bidder's financial offer; this was an hypothetical option presented in the financial analysis.

23. The level of concessionality for the Congaloshas contract is still to be settled but will be based on a target IRR around 10 per cent for the concessionaire. CAF will issue credits from their “vulnerable countries” portfolio, which are specifically designed to support projects that are classified in the Ecuadorian banking and financial system. CAF propose that GCF match their semi-concessional loan with a credit line of US$ 25 million at an interest rate of around one per cent.

24. CAF has the ambition to become a green financier and stretches their offer beyond standard practice in the Congaloshas case. For example, it is extremely rare that CAF would offer loans to projects with concessional terms above 10 per cent. The maximum concessional loan offered by CAF is US$ 25 million. Moreover, they consider decreasing their interest rate even further from the current rate and extending the maturity of the loan.

25. According to our assessment, the GCF eligibility criteria serve to address two major areas of relevance for shifting the energy use end towards a more efficient and flexible load. Efficiency improvements are achieved through the inclusion of an efficiency target for equipment, and the embedded energy supply further helps to cut the required electricity demand. However, the project could have been even more impactful in industries, business models to achieve higher targets through market-based instruments could potentially have further enhanced the project's impact, including how the recoustration outcome.

26. The Galapagos Climate Credit Line is clear and well designed, building on previous experiences (from the Refrigerator Renewable Program RENEGA). A barrier for these types of activities in Ecuador has been the lack of a credit line specifically targeted at energy efficiency. This loan will be developed with a blend of GCF concessional loan. CAF will sign a Term Sheet and an Agreement to ensure that the project is implemented within the agreed timeframe, and that it benefits end beneficiaries, and will offer 12-11 per cent interest rate as opposed to 17 per cent for the decreasing IRR on the Congaloshas contract. The Galapagos Climate Credit Line has a gate (presumably 15.2 per cent) but without GCF funding it is likely that the LFs in Galapagos will offer no credit at all for transitioning to more energy efficient equipment. The activity entails capacity building and awareness raising, which are carried out through the implementation of Components 2 and 3 in, in which value chain development and ecosystem are being carried out. As such, the project is expected to have a significant impact on the sector.


---
**Page 150**
---

Component A can support resource optimization and energy efficiency in the design of buildings. In addition, the energy optimization of the food value chains in Component B could be linked to the GCF opportunities. These possibilities have been communicated to the AE and may potentially be included as part of the project.

In terms of grid-connected equipment, a study on grid stability is underway. Although there are regulations to control that equipment does not cause disturbance or imbalance in the distribution network, an eventual law derived from this work needs to be developed prior to first disbursement.

**Component 2 - Building ecosystem resilience for livelihoods in the Galapagos (total cost USD 36 M; GCF 100%)**

* **Component A:** Strengthen Galapagos farmers’ and small-scale fishers’ adaptive capacity in order to increase local food production through the provision of reimbursable non-remunerative inputs, such as seeds, tools, and equipment. This will help communities promote more efficient value chains and a blue circular economy; foster the protection and restoration of ecosystems; improve access to markets for Galapagos products (e.g., fish, cacao, including tourism); and strengthen the decision-making frameworks related to ecosystem conservation.

In the case of agriculture, the project will increase the adaptive capacity of farmers by achieving participatory and integral farm planning with producers, technical assistance, and concessional inputs. The project will also provide training for Galapagos farmers on appropriate materials and equipment. Income diversification opportunities for resilient climate production systems will be created through the development of value chains.

The programme will implement three main ecosystem-based adaptation measures to improve the resilience and adaptive capacity of the Galapagos small-scale fisheries: improving the design, management, and governance of marine protected areas; implementing conservation and fishery management objectives; restoring the ecological function of overexploited habitats; and promoting sustainable livelihoods for the local community through a climate-smart approach to small-scale fishing and aquaculture. And diversifying fishers’ livelihoods based on their pillars: innovation and technology, circular economy, and social capital. The project will also contribute to the development of the Galapagos tuna fishery.

This component will also improve the knowledge of Galapagos government staff and vulnerable farmers on climate change issues and climate-resilient agricultural best practices through activities such as farmer-to-farmer schools and training workshops. The generation of access to information and knowledge for decision-making will be prioritized.

Our assessment concludes that the activities will lead to an increased, sustainable climate resilience in Galapagos small-scale fisheries. If implemented with adequate resources, activities will effectively bring about change. For example, it is considered that the activities related to proactive prevention of the spread of invasive species and marine zoning for ecosystem-based management are likely to be effective. The measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions are designed as no-regret measures given the uncertainty and unpredictability of future climate conditions.

It is also valuable that monitoring of the ecosystems’ vulnerability to changes in climate is enhanced in the Galapagos, as this will contribute to long-term sustainability and development.

The activities proposed are aimed at improving data availability, carbon sequestration, biodiversity (seeds, fishers, tourism operators), establishing better institutional conditions and planning tools that incorporate climate change for managing ecosystems and agriculture landscapes.


---
**Page 151**
---

### Component 3 - Sustainability mechanisms for climate change and low-emission livelihoods

#### Total cost: USD 3.52M (GCF USD 4.83M or 97%)

- **Component 1**: Capacity building of local livelihoods and population through education and communication programmes, to increase the capacities of key institutions and individuals to face climate change in policy and planning instruments, thus empowering their decision-making.
  
  - Activities under this component will include: implementation of an ecosystem certification programme for the Galapagos Islands; strengthening the tourism value chain; strengthening the educational system of Galapagos to provide quality education to face climate change and promote sustainable development; strengthening knowledge and fostering engagement with local communities, including indigenous peoples, in the design of solutions; and strengthening institutional and regulatory systems for climate responsive planning.

- **Component 2**: Implementation of the proposed activities among the fisheries and foreseen beneficiaries is assessed as medium to low. Small-scale fishers' associations have been consulted on the proposed activities and their potential impact on livelihoods and ecosystems. Letters of commitment from these associations will be provided, stating their interest in participating in and promoting programme activities. A behavioural insights analysis will be conducted with the aim of identifying key drivers for participation among small-scale fisheries stakeholders in the programme activities.

- **Component 3**: Sustainability mechanisms for climate change and low-emission livelihoods

#### Total cost: USD 3.52M (GCF USD 4.83M or 97%)

- **Main actors**:
  - The main direct stakeholders are the local governments (Government Council of the Special Zone of Galapagos), tourism operators, small-scale fisheries and the education sector (schools and universities). More than 670 beneficiaries from the tourism sector will be directly involved in the implementation of this component. This includes 125 hotels, 48 restaurants, 30 tour operators, 90 agricultural sector farmers, 1,000 small-scale fishers and 1,000 students.
  
- **Main indirect stakeholders**:
  - The main indirect stakeholders are the Galapagos National Park (GNP), which is responsible for the management of protected areas in the islands; the Galapagos Marine Reserve (GMR); and the local communities, including indigenous peoples.

- **Actions will be implemented in the four populated islands (Floreana, Isabela, San Cristóbal, and Santa Cruz) High Ecological Value Areas (HEVA), and the Galapagos Marine Reserve (GMR).** The GNP is responsible for the management of protected areas in the islands.


---
**Page 152**
---

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### Impact potential: Medium

The project results in lower emissions from the centralized electricity system, from decentralized power supply and energy efficient measures.

- The project will result in a significant shift towards renewable energy in the electricity generation sector, with all new generating capacity based on renewable energy compared to zero in the business as usual scenario. Regarding the decentralized renewable energy and energy efficiency measures, this project will contribute to a more sustainable and reliable energy situation at the same time contributing to their local economic development.

The programme will enhance 10,500 hectares of agricultural areas, 1,560 hectares of forested land, and 338,000 ha of marine ecosystems. The total beneficiaries of the programme are the total population (33,000 people) and the more than 270,000 annual tourists. These will benefit from a significant reduction in greenhouse gas emissions and a significant energy footprint. These beneficiaries will further benefit from the distributed power generation system that is being implemented.

Tourism is an important backbone of the local economy and the islands ecosystems, including its fauna, are important attractions. For this reason, it is essential to strive to keep the tourism industry sustainable and climate neutral as far as possible, which this project


---
**Page 153**
---

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

#### Scale: Medium to high

The project addresses a shift towards renewable energy, local financing of sustainable climate resilient investments and introduces methods for marine management and integrated coastal zone management (ICZM). The Galapagos Islands are an example of an unsustainable import-dependent local economy, with significant net GHG emissions due to imports of fuel and food, as well as unsustainable tourism management that challenges the very ecosystems being protected. In this project, the focus is on building a deeper understanding of integrating climate resilience and low emission codes of conduct in the various sectors of the Galapagos Islands economy. The project also addresses the energy supply that is complemented with battery storage: a game-changer for potentially reducing or eliminating diesel fuel subsidies. This will lead to significant improvements in sustainable energy and energy efficiency, which together with the inclusion and capacity-building of financial instruments, will support the transition towards a low-carbon economy.

Component 2 in particular aims at building up of successful investments. Component 3 in particular aims to fortify regulations, educational curricula and ecotourism certification schemes that will support the shift.


### 3.3 Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: High

The project tackles both mitigation and adaptation aspects of climate change and works to enforce the sustainability of relevant economic sectors in the Galapagos Islands first and foremost through the introduction of sustainable business models, including proportions of the local economy. In particular, the project addresses long-term aspects of the challenge of reducing GHG emissions from the energy sector by developing economically sustainable business models where practices are developed for sustainable marine zoning, cost-effective renewable energy supply and battery storage in the Galapagos Islands. The Energy provision in the Galapagos Islands has been heavily subsidised through diesel subsidies. The government is committed to phasing out these subsidies over time. In the particular case of the Galapagos, government savings from diesel will be supporting the general sustainability agenda of the country. In addition, the AI will bring the need for specific support for the Galapagos Islands in terms of climate change adaptation and mitigation whereby funds can be reinvested in the local climate ambitions, such as for example PESGMG, the maritime carbon fund or the conservation for agricultural and fisheries. As such, sustainability is supported by the cross-cutting approach.


### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: Medium

The Galapagos Islands are remote and highly characterized by extreme climate events. Limited financing and high dependency on imported food and fuel increase climate vulnerability of local communities and ecosystems in the Galapagos Islands, including tourism. The tourism industry has become an essential part of the local economy, and as such contributes significantly to the anthropogenic impact on local livelihoods and ecosystems. The local community is increasingly dependent on tourism for income generation rather than using their ecosystems and nature resources as opposed to earning money from exposing them to tourism.


---
**Page 154**
---

3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The programme is developed in strategic alignment with the policy objectives of the Government of Ecuador and the vision of climate change and development. More specifically for Ecuador the programme also adheres to local plans and strategies. Notably, with COVID-19, some local institutions have been weakened by the pandemic and have lost staff. The programme can capacitate these institutions and help maintain some of the crucial activities. A new nature reserve in the Galapagos has just recently launched (at COP26). The Government of Ecuador is committed to protecting its biodiversity and intends to manage these climate change is therefore a high priority, both nationally and internationally. The project will contribute to national capacity-building with the incorporation of international experts, given the exclusive high value ecosystems of the Galapagos Islands.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

Of the GCF financing 39 per cent will go towards mitigation activities. This results in a credit of USD 105 million for the distributed renewable components and energy efficiency in the GCF portfolio. The cost effectiveness for adaptation projects is high at USD 1,027 (MCO). However, this can be justified by the remoteness of the project location and low population density. The average annual income per capita in the Galapagos Islands is USD 3,584, which equates to 1.8 times higher than on the mainland and salaries are 60 per cent higher than on the mainland.

The economic efficiency of the mitigation interventions is good. The estimated economic internal rate of return for the centralised renewable energy intervention is 45.2 per cent, for the distributed renewable components and energy efficiency it is 37.9 per cent and for the energy efficiency it is 17.89 per cent, all above the discount rate of 12 per cent used for Peru. Economic returns were not assessed for the adaptation interventions.

In terms of appropriateness of concessionality and financial adequacy, the AE provided with and without GCF financial scenario analysis for all the components under consideration.

Appropriateness of concessionality: The distributed renewable components and energy efficiency have a negative net present value (NPV) and an IRR below the weighted average cost of capital without the GCF contribution. This will increase from 72.2 per cent to 73.80 per cent thanks to the GCF contribution, which is still below the threshold of 100 per cent according to the AE. The GCF level of concessionality therefore seems appropriate for the component.

For the distributed renewable components and energy efficiency the AE estimates IRRs of around 7.7 per cent without GCF funding and 9.86 per cent with GCF funding, still being below the discount rate of 12 per cent used by the AE over a period of 8 years for the GCF contribution. These components do not exhibit sufficient profitability to be fully financed through concessional loans. The proposed use of grant is therefore appropriate according to component loan for component 1.2.1 seems appropriate.

The climate-smart agriculture activities 2.1.2 and 2.1.3 exhibit negative NPV and an IRR of 80 per cent without GCF funding, which is still below the 10 per cent discount rate and the payback is still standing at 6 years. The proposed use of grant is the appropriate financing mechanism for these activities as it will finance the interventions selected by the AE to calculate profitability (paragraph 38ff of the funding proposal).

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The proposal is classified as Category A primarily because of the sensitive environment of the Galapagos Islands. Development in the islands always increases the chance of any non-native species being introduced into the archipelago which would present a very serious risk to its unique ecosystems.

The project involves activities that are likely to have significant impacts on terrestrial and marine sectors (energy, agriculture, forestry, fishery, tourism), some of the activities involve direct land use change. The proposal includes restoration of forest, reforestation, rehabilitation of degraded lands, restoration of forest, restoration of coral reef, removal of invasive species, restoration of remnant vegetation, creation of protected areas, and establishment of a marine protected area.

On the other hand, some of the environmental and social co-benefits of the proposal include: protection of biodiversity, conservation of natural resources, promotion of agroecology, restoration and protection of marine and terrestrial ecosystems; job creation. Healthy food environment, water security and increasing resilience to climate change.

An updated version of the Gender Strategy (GS) and the Social Management Framework (SMF) incorporating safeguards to address sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual harassment (SEAH) and a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) have been developed in accordance with GCF requirements. The Secretariat and revision by the AE prior to the proposal's consideration by the Board. In relation to indigenous peoples, the AE notes that while indigenous peoples are not key stakeholders in the Galapagos Islands development context, they may be affected by the project activities. The policy does not need to be applied as indigenous peoples are not key stakeholders in the Galapagos Islands development context.

The proposal is consistent with the GCF Gender Policy and the GS and SMF incorporate gender equality through provisions made in the national constitution, including a law on the prevention and eradication of gender-based violence. The country has also ratified international instruments such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women, and regional conventions such as the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment and Eradication of Violence Against Women (Istanbul Convention). The country has established a National Commission for example the National Agenda on Women and Gender Equality. At the institutional level, the National Council for Women is responsible for coordinating gender equality issues in any mainstreamed in policies and institutions. Although the country has put in place a legal and

### Gender equality and prevailing gender stereotypes

The general context of gender issues in Ecuador is described as follows:

- **Gender roles**: Women are responsible for childcare and domestic tasks while men are involved in agricultural activities.
- **Access to resources**: Women have limited access to credit, land titles, and other financial instruments. They also face challenges in accessing markets and technology.
- **Economic participation**: Women earn less than men and often work in informal sectors or as unpaid family workers.

The programme aims to address these issues by:

1. **Improving access to resources**:
   - Providing microloans for energy efficiency technologies, farmers, and fishers from local financial institutions through the target group.
2. **Strengthening women's participation**: The action plan includes gender-related activities such as access to microloans for energy efficiency technologies, farmers, and fishers.

The programme also highlights:

- **Priorities highlighted by participants**:
  - Access to credit
  - Access to markets and technology
  - Access to training and education

The action plan is developed based on the assessment conducted. It includes gender-related activities such as access to microloans for energy efficiency technologies, farmers, and fishers from local financial institutions through the target group.

### Action Plan

- **Activities**: The action plan outlines specific activities with clear timelines and responsibilities.
- **Training and education**: The need for technical assistance is addressed through training education for rural women in areas such as agroforestry, energy efficiency technologies, biodiversity restoration, and repair and maintenance of energy-efficient technologies. There are no gender-related activities specifically targeted at households and businesses.

### Implementation

The programme aims to focus on women without considering their specific needs or challenges. The relevance of some gender-related activities is not clear in the action plan. The performance indicators provided are not well defined, and not all targets are disaggregated by sex.

## Credit Risk: The GCF and AE shall take the credit risk of the concessionary SPV to be established under the Galapagos Loan Agreement (see Component 1.1). The AE will conduct due diligence on the project and the borrower (Concession SPV) and will complete the credit rating process for the Concession SPV. Comfort is derived from co-financing with GCF, which has a high credit rating. The Concession SPV is not exposed to the sovereigns' credit risk. The government of Ecuador has below investment grade credit rating; it is rated as Cad by Moody's.

### Impact: 27,525 people will benefit (60% of the Galapagos population) as direct beneficiaries and 761,974 people as indirect beneficiaries which include 35,080 tourists. The number of direct beneficiaries may be different based on the annual number of distinct beneficiaries may be different based on the combined inflow of tourists and possibility of same tourist visiting in different years.

### Project cost: The project cost is estimated at USD $124 million. The process for selecting the EPC contractor as well as the O&M contractor. The tender winner (Granaroli/Erren) expects that the required initial investment will be around 30% of the total project cost, which is USD $37 million. Hence, approximately 30% of the project cost needs to be raised as equity from the sponsor's (tender winner) own sources. The final figure will be known once the EPC contractor is appointed. It is recommended that the GCF provide a loan for the initial investment and the tender process and is limited to 25% of the project cost as estimated in the funding proposal.

### Project schedule: The project is expected to close for the project cost within [24] months of signing the Galapagos Loan Agreement between the AE and Concession SPV. If the project is delayed and the financial close is not achieved within the time period, the GCF will be exposed to a delay risk.

### Demand risk: The success of the GCL depends on the EPC's ability to support LFs to source commercial financing for the project. The GCF will provide technical assistance to monitor and monitor the project impact is achieved. There is in low demand of the loan due to (i) resistance to change the traditional way of farming and fishing from the beneficiaries; (ii) inability of the LFs to access credit from banks; (iii) lack of awareness among the LFs about the lack of commercial incentives for cruise companies to invest in Digital Positioning System; and (iv) lack of awareness on the impact of COVID-19 related restrictions and consequent non-reactivation of tourism sector in the Island. To partially mitigate this demand risk, the grant is going to be used for the awareness raising and technical assistance activities.

### Project viability and concessionality: For the Galapagos GCF will require to provide a high concessional rate to the Concession SPV. The interest rate of AE will be provided to the public financial institutions (IEF/COMHIS/CNF) at a blended rate, which will then be used by the LFs to access credit from the IEF/COMHIS/CNF. If concessional rates are not provided, but the IEF/COMHIS will grant similar terms and tenor that they receive from GCF. The AE is required to ensure that the concessionality of GCL financing is passed on by the LFs to the beneficiaries

#### 10. CAF will co-work with the World Wildlife Fund (WWF), the Ecuadorian Forests, Environment and Water Ministry (MEFEW), and the Canadian Financial Corporation (CFN), namely the Ecuadorian Development Bank, as EEs of the programme.

#### 11. The EE's will engage government partners such as the Government Council of the Special Economic Zone for Agriculture, Livestock, Forestry, Mining, Energy, Water and Tourism, Ministry of Energy, and Non-Renewable Natural Resources, and Ministry of Tourism in implementing the programme. These entities are responsible for the implementation and application of the local government legal framework and are part of the Sectoral Technical Committee (STC) of the Ministry of Environment, Water and Ecological Transition, as the national designated authority, with a representative from each entity holding a leading role in the Steering Committee.

#### 12. The Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established in the Galapagos Islands and will have a dedicated team to guarantee all components and activities are carried out according to the programme design. It will oversee the monitoring and evaluation activities (covered by the GCF), as well as the implementation of the project, ensuring that it is on time and within budget. The PMU will have a Programme Coordinator; a Monitoring and Evaluation Specialist; an Environmental and Social Safeguards Specialist; a Financial Management/ Auditing Specialist; an Environmental and Social Safeguards Specialist; and an Accounting Assistant. The coordinator will be responsible for the overall coordination of the programme, working closely with the sectoral businesses (private and public) and administrative (legal, procurement, human resources) entities.

#### 13. CAF will provide technical and administrative backstopping to the PMU to ensure results-oriented management and proper administration of funds. It will maintain programme accountability for all activities carried out under the loan tranche. The PMU will be subject to annual audits undertaken by internationally certified auditors. All functions will also be subject to regular monitoring, evaluation, and reporting (MER) and approval of annual work plans based on consultations with EEs and other necessary parties in the programme.

The EEs will be responsible for implementing commercial practices and procedures in all operations financed with GCF resources. In the provision of financing, CAF will perform a due diligence of the EEs and the EE's and carefully assess their capacity to execute the project.

#### 15. FAO and WWF will ensure the coordinated execution of activities under the grant tranche. WWF will be responsible for the implementation of the activities related to agriculture and livestock, and WWF will develop the activities related to support fisheries, the restoration and conservation activities in marine and terrestrial HAVELs, and the sustainability activities in the Galapagos Islands. FAO will provide technical assistance with cross-cutting characteristics. FAO and WWF will provide technical support to the Mitigation Action Plan (MAP) and will ensure that all project activities are of high quality in their operations and are accountable for executing the projects according to the principles and modalities established by GCF, including but not limited to: independent monitoring, independent evaluation, and financial audits of all activities funded by GCF.

# Results monitoring and reporting

The proposed project is contributing to a transformational change towards a self-sufficient island system in which local livelihoods are developed under a low-carbon model and enhanced capacity to adapt to climate change.

- The project will contribute to the active mitigation impact of 584,168 tCO2eq over project life span of 25 years and reach 33,000 direct and 728,974 indirect beneficiaries. The impacts are expected to be achieved through the implementation of new technologies and will allow for consistent measurement of results during the project implementation.

- The Theory of Change is designed to clearly show the logical pathways for generating results by addressing the barriers that prevent the achievement of the desired outcomes. The relevant assumptions have been also made and explanation of the ways barriers has addressed has been provided.

- The logical framework of the programme was designed in line with the GCF RMF/PMF and following the programme ToC. The logical framework has selected relevant impact, outcome and output indicators to measure results areas that the programme is contributing to, as well as the programmes specific activities.

- The implementation table has been prepared in Annex 5 and the M&I plan in Annex 11.


## Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 November 2016 (for “AMA”), and it became effective on 19 March 2018.

- The AE has not provided a legal opinion certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals necessary for the implementation of the proposed programme. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board, the AE has obtained all such approvals and provided a legal opinion certificate confirming from the AE in form and substance satisfactory to GCF confirming that all final internal approvals by the AE have been obtained for the implementation of the proposed programme.

- The proposed programme will be implemented in the Republic of Ecuador, a country in which GCF does not operate with protectionary immunity. This means that GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed by the AE and reflected in the legal opinion certificate annexed to the NDA on 25 April 2016. Discussions are still in progress and the last communication was on 3 October 2018.

- The Board of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities against GCF in Ecuador, due to the fact that GCF is not covered by any relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF are made only after GCF has obtained necessary protectionary protections against litigation and expropriation in this country, or has been given an appropriate waiver of privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP185

## Proposal name: Climate Change: The New Evolutionary Challenge for the Galapagos

### Accredited entity: Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF) / Development Bank of Latin America

### Country/(ies): Ecuador

### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel¹

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

#### 1.1.1 Overview

The proposed project requests USD 65.3 million from the GCF for a total project value of USD 117.6 million to support an integrated approach of cross-sectoral and cross-cutting interventions in the Galapagos Islands, including climate change adaptation measures, small-scale renewable energy generation projects, sustainable land use management practices, conservation measures, and nature and biodiversity conservation, placed in the context of a site of global importance.

The approach taken by the project, of integrating a range of cross-sectoral and cross-cutting measures at territorial level, is novel and should be followed and evaluated closely during the implementation phase. If implemented and monitored and reported on as requested successfully, it holds the promise of creating positively reinforcing actions across sectors, which have the potential to create positive feedback loops between different actors with different mindsets, achieving beneficial interlinkages and self-reinforcing success stories.

This approach comes with a price, however. The impact potential of a project of this nature could be limited by the lack of sufficient resources for implementation in the confined territory over which the implementation will occur is expected to alleviate this risk.

#### 1.1.2 Climate impact

The proposed project is cross-cutting, and the climate impact is generally clearly set out and accepted. The project aims at reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reductions totaling 584,169 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) over the project's lifetime, largely through the switch to solar energy generation using photovoltaics (PV), as well as other renewable energy sources such as wind and geothermal. Climate model trends and future projection models indicate that communities and economic sectors in the Galapagos Islands are likely to be affected by climate change impacts including increased surface temperatures, ocean acidification, sea level rise, increased frequency of rainfall, and


---
**Page 164**
---

more intense El Niño/Southern Oscillation (ENSO) events. The adaptation impact of the project will be derived through enhancing climate resilience in 90,000 hectares of agricultural land, restoring 1,500 hectares of indigenous Scalesia forest that provides important hydrological services to the catchment area, and improving coastal management in the southern part (períos) of the highlands in the cool season), and improved marine management involving 138,000 km² of maritime protected areas.

On the marine side, the previous submission of the funding proposal left open significant questions relating to the climate-additivity of the adaptation activities, in particular in relation to the potential impacts on the recruitment and survival of commercially important species. For example, there was a lack of clarity in the submitted proposal regarding which climatic parameters were having a negative impact on coral reefs in the archipelago and how this impact would be exacerbated by climate change. It is not clear whether the resilience that would be enhanced because the planted corals would be more resistant to climate change or whether the resilience would be enhanced through the planting of the same amount that was lost in the past.

Similarly for fisheries, the proposal was not clear on how much of the production side of the fishery would be affected by climate vulnerability of the shellfish and finfish stocks in the archipelago and what specific climatic parameters present a risk for these resources. The proposal also did not provide any information about whether the impacts described in the funding proposal of their spread being driven by climate change, as the basis for using GCF funding for this project.

The A.A. Corporación Andina de Fomento – Andean Development Corporation (CAF), addressed these issues substantively and to the satisfaction of the independent TAP in its response to the questions posed by the TAP. The CAF noted that: “the outcomes of multiple drivers’ interactions in Galápagos’ ecosystems remain unknown” and that further research is needed on how climate change will affect the fisheries sector in the project. The present version of the funding proposal was updated to include these issues and other responses were provided to the questions posed by the independent TAP.

The TAP also noted that for some marine species, it is precautionary approach should be applied, even in the absence of a full understanding of how climate change will affect them. This is particularly relevant given the Galápagos Islands’ dependence on the fisheries and tourism sectors, as well as on livelihoods that rely on natural resource systems.

The TAP also notes the conservative assumptions regarding the GHG emission savings potential with interventions in the agriculture, forestry and land use sector only being considered as co-benefits. This provides a valuable safety cushion. Nevertheless, the TAP recommends that these co-benefits be assessed and that any emission reductions assessed properly and integrated fully into the results framework of the project.

The TAP has proposed uses data from meteorological stations for analysing historical climate trends, comparing them with other sources such as satellite imagery and remote sensing products, which are validated against observed meteorological datasets. The funding proposal states that “pre-project baseline data” is available in the form of a 1980-2017 period (supplementary figure 2 of appendix 5). It shows mean annual precipitation and temperature series as well as monthly precipitation series for the Galápagos Islands between 1981 and 2017 compared with the CHLSEA, ERA and MODIS datasets, and some charts show significant discrepancies, mostly regarding precipitation observations.

provided suggests that precipitation (i.e., rainfall including mist/hog) is heavily influenced by the ENSO and also by the migration of the Interdecadal Convergence Zone. The El Niño has become invigorated: its greater wetting ability with much increased rainfall and bias towards wet seasons in the Sahel region, and its drying effect on the dry season rainfall, becoming a dominant feature during the La Niña, which is also associated with reduced rainfall over the Sahel region, an area of globally important crops. The ETA-led droughts lead to reduction of crop productivity and hence affects livestock.

14. The feasibility study defines changes in particle movements and sea surface temperature (SST) increases as main threats to the sustainability of shallow coral populations. These are associated with increased sedimentation, which is compounded by coral degradation as a result of unsustainable tourism. The description in the funding proposal appears to be based on the feasibility review, but lacks the description and validation of the models that were used to arrive at the climate projections.

14. The models used to project future precipitation scenarios have the same problem. The lack of representation of the observed drying trend is a major concern, as it is not captured by models compared with the observed drying trend. The high degree of uncertainty regarding precipitation is recognised in the proposal.

15. It is proposed that climate change impacts related to agricultural practices and ecosystem restoration and conservation would be beneficial independently of the evolution of precipitation patterns. This is because increased rainfall will lead to drier conditions by reducing evaporation, and increased agro-biodiversity would enhance resilience under both drier and wetter conditions. Crop diversification involving resilient varieties and species would also reduce vulnerability. However, it seems that only a few climate parameters change only a fraction of the crops are affected, thus reducing the impact and making it difficult to justify the proposed activities.

(Develop and implement water collection and water management systems for climate-resilient food production) would be beneficial independently of precipitation changes, as they have a more pronounced rainfall distribution between wet and dry/dry seasons), mostly by the need to improve water storage.

17. Regarding the relationship between non-indigenous invasive species and climate change, the proposal describes the potential expansion of the macro algae Gaudiera chemistries linked to increased SSTs and reduced precipitation in the Sahel region. The expansion of gava (Pulmon pusilla) under all climate scenarios. However, the proposal fails to demonstrate that this is a consequence of climate change or that the current distribution seems more likely to be a consequence of agricultural land use. The proposal recognises that the majority of the proposed activities are located in areas where actual gava dominated patches that currently are located within agricultural areas.

18. The proposal states the whole Galapagos population (2,000 inhabitants) would indirectly benefit from the project. However, it is not clear how this applies to beneficiaries for most of the activities and assumes that concessional loans, ecosystem restoration, institutional strengthening and education activities would benefit the whole population. **Page 167**
---

The promotion of centralized PV energy generation would reduce the carbon footprint of the electrical grid that serves Santa Cruz island, with a population of 15,000.

The project is estimated to benefit over 21,000 Galapagos residents, who will be targeted for investments and technical assistance. The project's promotion of renewable energy generation will also have indirect benefits for people during the 5-year project duration, as counted in the funding proposal as indirect beneficiaries. The independent TAP assessment considers that the project has a medium-high impact potential.

The title of the proposed project, referring to the evolutionary challenge posed by the Galapagos Islands by climate change, is apt and correctly reflects both scale and the effort that will be required to achieve it. The Government of Ecuador and all executing entities (EEs) should be congratulated for bringing this project forward, since it provides a unique opportunity to protect one of the world's most important natural heritage assets of worldwide cultural as well as historical importance. Given the nature of the project, the paradigm shift potential is high.

On the mitigation side, the Consolymus solar PV and storage project will act as an enabler for further renewable energy to be installed on the archipelago, which will ultimately allow the islands to become more resilient in the face of climate change. This will be supported by the credit lines, which can be expected to create a small-scale renewable and recovery economy that could provide employment opportunities for local clients over many years, even after the end of the proposed GCF project. These are almost certainly going to have lasting impacts. In response to questions from the independent TAP on the issue of battery life, it is important to note that there are already existing programmes that deal with adequate management of batteries throughout their life cycle.

On the resilience side, impacts on biodiversity, nature and communities have high potential to be paradigm shifting, generating new ways of interacting with nature and preventing further loss of species. The project will also contribute to food security. Shifting to local food production through agro- and fish-processing involves a major shift, because 80 per cent of the population is currently dependent on imported foods. This would facilitate sustainable revenue streams out of which long-term investments could be made in climate change resilience.

Adoption of innovative technologies, such as the use of fog catchers to enhance water supply in areas with unreliable rains, and dynamic positioning systems that enable dive boats to stay offshore for longer periods of time, are also paradigm shifting. These innovations would unlock private-sector investment. For dynamic positioning systems, this could occur through a combination of public-private partnerships and/or through the mechanism by which Ecuador is participating, which can facilitate equity investment in companies seeking to sell this technology to tourism operators – if and when the concept is proven.

# Sustainable development potential: Scale High

The proposed project pursues an integrated, territory-based approach to addressing a range of challenges in a defined area across several sectors. While this increases complexity substantially, it also provides the ability to generate changes that will prove long-lasting, because they are based on the creation of new economic activities and social structures with the potential for the creation of a virtuous circle in which environmental improvements reinforce each other.

Beyond the investment operations, activities such as strengthening the education system, research into the multidimensional challenges imposed by climate change and human activity (e.g., land use changes), and the promotion of local markets can generate long-lasting impacts that go well beyond the boundaries of the proposed project.

The project will also have a positive impact on the credit lines; community engagement and the creation of an innovation lab, together with strengthening the value chain, have the potential to effect long-term positive changes for both financial institutions and the communities they serve.

In relation to supporting climate-resilient agriculture and adding value through agri-food processing, the project will support the development of a local market by creating local markets for food products including in the tourism sector. Increased consumption of diversified fresh produce will also improve health and nutrition, as well as food security, for residents of the proposed project area.

Although the funding proposal says that the project would enhance the local coffee market by supporting the production of "high-quality Arabica coffee", the related activity would include 100 per cent of the local farms that exclusively produce coffee (20 farms; 640 ha), and 45 per cent of the smaller farms that produce coffee mixed with other crops (137 farms; 890 ha). This is a very small proportion of the total area under coffee, which is inherent to agroforestry systems: it does not contribute to enhancing biodiversity, and it is more difficult to manage without the use of chemical pesticides and herbicides. Moreover, the


---
**Page 169**
